[^fn-p0090-1]: (*) Adolf Hildebrand (b. 1847), the sculptor, who executed the Brahms monument at Meiningen, the relief figures on the tombs of the two Herzogenbergs, and the bust photographed in this book.
[^fn-p0090-2]: (†) Richard Pohl (1826-1896), the voluble Wagner apostle, was bringing out a series, *Æsthetic Letters to a Young Musician*, in the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, with the title *In which Style ought we to Compose?*
[^fn-p0090-3]: (‡) The keys of three pianoforte pieces, published in 1879 as Op. 76 (Nos. 1 and 2 of book i., and No. 7 of book ii.), which Brahms had played to them when at Arnoldstein.
[^fn-p0091-1]: (*) Frau Werzer, manageress of the hotel and post-office at Pörtschach, with whom Brahms was on excellent terms. ‘Miss Post-Office’ was the name he gave her daughter.
[^fn-p0091-2]: (†) See Letters 39-41.
[^fn-p0092-1]: (*) Brahms had been invited to the Hamburg Musical Festival (September 25-28), on the occasion of the Golden Jubilee of the Philharmonic Society. He conducted his second symphony on the third evening, and called at Leipzig on his way back.
[^fn-p0092-2]: (†) The Violin concerto, Op. 77, and the Violin sonata, Op. 78, were commenced in the summer of 1878, and finished late in the autumn.
[^fn-p0092-3]: (‡) A symphony composed in 1832, and performed in the following year at Prague and Leipzig, which was revived in 1878 for a short time.
[^fn-p0092-4]: (§) Marie Wilt (1833-1891), prima donna at the Court Theatre, Vienna, who took the part of Brünnhilde at Leipzig.
[^fn-p0093-1]: (*) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0093-2]: (†) Brahms, Op. 40 and Op. 60.
[^fn-p0093-3]: (‡) Director of the Gewandhaus committee.
[^fn-p0093-4]: (§) The jubilee to celebrate the fiftieth anniversary of Frau Schumann's first public appearance (at a concert given in the Gewandhaus by Caroline Verlthaler, a pianist from Graz) on October 20, 1878. A concert in her honour had been arranged at the Gewandhaus on the 14th, consisting of Schumann's works, at which a golden laurel-wreath was to be presented to her. Brahms was not able to be present, as he was conducting at Breslau on the 22nd (his second symphony), and playing in his A major quartet on the 24th.
[^fn-p0094-1]: (*) Marie Fillunger.
[^fn-p0095-1]: (*) ‘Oh, have pity on my misery, and send the longed-for Intermezzi!’ Brahms had played some of his new Capriccios and Intermezzi (Op. 76) to the Herzogenbergs on his visit to Arnoldstein in September, and the above is a reminiscence of the Intermezzo in A minor, second part. It may be taken as a proof of Frau von Herzogenberg’s remarkably quick ear and retentive memory.
[^fn-p0095-2]: (†) Refers to the song in the previous letter. Brahms sent this particular piece later, with the inscription *Romanze für 2 zarte Frauenstimmen und 2 zarten Frauenzimmern gewidmet.*
[^fn-p0096-1]: (*) Capriccio, Op. 76, No. 2.
[^fn-p0096-2]: (†) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0097-1]: (*) Op. 76, No. 1.
[^fn-p0097-2]: (†) Op. 76, No. 5.
[^fn-p0098-1]: (*) The first symphony, which was to be performed again at the Gewandhaus on New Year’s Day.
[^fn-p0098-2]: (†) Edvard Grieg (1843-1907), Norwegian composer.
[^fn-p0098-3]: (‡) The *Allgemeine Musikalische Zeitung*, published by the firm Rieter-iedermann, was founded in 1866, and withdrawn in 1882 ; edited from 1869 onwards by Friedrich Chrysander.
[^fn-p0099-1]: (*) The note-paper bears the motto : ‘Postponed is not abandoned.’
[^fn-p0100-1]: (*) *Cf.* Letter 50, note.
[^fn-p0100-2]: (†) The concerto for violin, for which Joachim had provided fingering and bowing marks. Brahms had written in the autumn saying that his first impulse had been to ‘offer his fingers’ for Joachim’s concert in Vienna, and keep back the violin concerto ; but his disinclination for concert-playing was too deep-rooted, and he had grown used to playing with himself as sole audience. Yet he hated to think of Joachim’s playing in Austria, while he ‘stood there doing nothing,’ and the only alternative was to conduct the Violin concerto. Would it be true hospitality to send him the score with a proper copy of the solo part ? The middle movements had been discarded (‘they were the best, of course’), but he was putting in a ‘feeble adagio.’ ‘We might as well give them the pleasure at Leipzig,’ he adds ; ‘we could hold a consultation here at the piano.’ The result of the consultation was exceedingly favourable, and the new work was able to be included in the concert programme for New Year’s Day at Leipzig. Brahms went to Berlin on December 18, and from there to Leipzig with Joachim.
[^fn-p0102-1]: (*) The manuscript was the property of Messrs. Härtel (*cf.* Letter 16).
[^fn-p0103-1]: (*) Joachim played the Violin concerto on January 14 in Vienna. He then went to England without Brahms, and played it twice by request at the Crystal Palace. Meeting Brahms again, they played it together at Bremen, Hamburg, and Berlin.
[^fn-p0104-1]: (*) The post-card is missing.
[^fn-p0104-2]: (†) *Cf.* Letter 1, note.
[^fn-p0106-1]: (*) Ferdinand Thieriot (b. 1838), composer, and at that time ‘artistic director’ of the Steiermark *Musikverein* at Graz. He was a countryman of Brahms’s, and, like him, a pupil of Eduard Marxsen.
[^fn-p0106-2]: (†) Op. 76 had now been published by Simrock.
[^fn-p0106-3]: (‡) Twelve German sacred folk-songs for mixed chorus, Op. 18.
[^fn-p0106-4]: (§) Second Trio in F for Violin, Viola, and Violoncello, Op. 27.
[^fn-p0107-1]: (*) Op. 56.
[^fn-p0107-2]: (†) That is to say, pianoforte, not organ, fugues.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0108-1]: (*) Brahms was for over twenty years a member of the committee, formed in 1863, for distributing the stipends granted by the Austrian Government for the education and support of young musical talent. He had in this capacity ample opportunity of judging the masters and students of the Vienna Conservatorium. His conscientiousness is shown in the opinions expressed in his marginal notes, some of which Hanslick reproduced in the *Neue Freie Presse* of June 19, 1897. In 1884 he complained: ‘It is really disgraceful and inexcusable year by year to ruin beyond repair the little talent we have.’ But the circumstances are now considerably improved.
[^fn-p0109-1]: (*) Brahms sometimes took a whole house in the country for the sake of privacy, although he only used two rooms. The summer was his best working time.
[^fn-p0109-2]: (†) The silver wedding of the Emperor and Empress.
[^fn-p0109-3]: (‡) Refers to the time when Kirchner was organist at Winterthur.
[^fn-p0109-4]: (§) Brahms had been offered the post of *cantor* at St. Thomas’s, once held by Bach.
[^fn-p0109-5]: (‖) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0110-1]: (*) The motet sung at St. Thomas’s by the famous choir every Saturday is an old-established and very popular institution.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0110-2]: (†) Ernst Friedrich Richter (1808-1879), a distinguished theorist, follower of Moritz Hauptmann, was *cantor* at the choir school from 1868 until his death.
[^fn-p0112-1]: (*) Franz Magnus Böhme (1827-1898), theorist, published his *Altdeutsches Liederbuch*, a collection of songs and melodies, in 1877. This was followed in 1893-94 by a new edition of Ludwig Erk’s *Liederhort* in three volumes. Brahms had not a good word for either collection, and the German Volkslieder in seven numbers, which he published through Simrock, may be regarded as an artistic protest against Böhme and his method. Herzogenberg’s arrangements are those mentioned by his wife in Letter 58.
[^fn-p0114-1]: (*) Op. 86, No. 6.
[^fn-p0114-2]: (†) *Therese*, Op. 86, No. 1.
[^fn-p0114-3]: (‡) The Violin Sonata in G, Op. 78, which was completed in the summer of 1879.
[^fn-p0115-1]: (*) Ballads and Romances, Op. 75, dedicated to Julius Allgeyer, painter, engraver, and photographer, also Anselm Feuerbach’s biographer, whose friendship with Brahms dated from 1853.
[^fn-p0115-2]: (†) Op. 78, p. 20, bar 5.
[^fn-p0116-1]: (*) Violin and pianoforte sonata, Op. 78.
[^fn-p0116-2]: (†) Brahms had visited Joachim at Aigen, near Salzburg, in August, and had played through the sonata with him, the Herzogenbergs coming over from Berchtesgaden for the purpose.
[^fn-p0117-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s *String Trios*, Op. 27.
[^fn-p0117-2]: (†) *Cf.* Letter 62, note.
[^fn-p0117-3]: (‡) *Deutsche Volkslieder für vierstimmigen Chor gesetzt*, published in 1864 without opus number.
[^fn-p0117-4]: (§) A letter from some member of the committee,
[^fn-p0117-5]: (‖) S. H. Mosenthal, author of the poem *Deborah*. Brahms met him sometimes in society and in the Viennese restaurants.
[^fn-p0117-6]: (¶) ‘The grave is my delight.’
[^fn-p0118-1]: (*) At the Bach-Verein.
[^fn-p0118-2]: (†) *Neue Davidsbündler Tänze*, Op. 18.
[^fn-p0120-1]: (*) Robert Radecke (b. 1830), violinist, pianist, organ virtuoso, musical director, etc.
[^fn-p0120-2]: (†) Two Rhapsodies, Op. 79.
[^fn-p0121-1]: (*) Joachim played the Brahms violin concerto again in Vienna on February 3, after which the two started on a tour through Poland and Galicia.
[^fn-p0121-2]: (†) A night spent in recalling the second of the two Rhapsodies, in G minor.
[^fn-p0122-1]: (*) P. 15, bar 15 *et seq.*
[^fn-p0122-2]: (†) P. 17, bars 1-7.
[^fn-p0122-3]: (‡) P. 5, bars 19-23.
[^fn-p0122-4]: (§) Brahms had called at Leipzig on his way home from a concert-tour on the Rhine, and it was on this occasion that Frau von Herzogenberg became acquainted with the rhapsodies.
[^fn-p0122-5]: (‖) The performance of Brahms’s violin concerto on December 28 by Joachim ; of the 8 *Klavierstücke* on January 4 by Bülow ; the Haydn Variations and *Song of Destiny* at the Gewandhaus under Reinecke ; the G major sextet at the Gewandhaus Kammermusik and at the Riedel Verein ; and the cantata *Rinaldo* at the Paulus Verein.
[^fn-p0124-1]: (*) Edmund Astor, Herzogenberg's friend and publisher.
[^fn-p0124-2]: (†) The inscriptions over the two pieces in the manuscript are respectively : *Capriccio* (*presto agitato*) and *Molto passionato*.
[^fn-p0125-1]: (*) From Goethe’s *Faust.* ‘*Himmelsklarheit*’ should be ‘*Himmelsglut.*’
[^fn-p0127-1]: (*) Brahms had apparently said he would not mind going to Berchtesgaden as Herr Müller—that is, as a private person, without responsibilities.
[^fn-p0127-2]: (†) A slip of the pen for Bonn, where a festival was held, from May 2 to May 4, to celebrate the unveiling of the Schumann monument. Joachim played Brahms’s violin concerto, and was joint-director of the festival with Brahms.
[^fn-p0127-3]: (‡) On the notes <span class="small-caps">C, A, F, F, E, E</span>.
[^fn-p0128-1]: (*) Brahms was suffering from aural catarrh.
[^fn-p0129-1]: (*) Op. 74, No. 1. *Cf.* Letters 40 and 41.
[^fn-p0129-2]: (†) Louis Ehlert (1825-1884), composer and writer on music, had published an essay on Brahms in the June number of Rodenberg’s *Deutsche Rundschau*.
[^fn-p0129-3]: (‡) Ehlert had said: ‘Brahms’s music has no profile, only a full face. It lacks the strongly-marked features which stamp the expression absolutely. . . . My observations have led me to conclude that nothing is so persistently transmitted through many generations as gestures. . . . His variations have practically no resemblance left in their faces to those of Beethoven and Schumann, yet they occasionally make their bow and go out at the door in the same way.’
[^fn-p0130-1]: (*) P. 10, bars 3 *et seq.*
[^fn-p0131-1]: (*) Brahms was, however, thoroughly alarmed by his sudden deafness, fearing that he was doomed to the same fate as Beethoven. He left for Vienna immediately, having wired his friend Billroth to meet him at the station. Billroth was able to reassure him, and direct him to a specialist. To Brahms’s great annoyance, a report of his illness got into the papers through an indiscretion, and he found it a serious matter to reply to all his letters of sympathy.
[^fn-p0131-2]: (†) A second series of *Hungarian Dances* for pianoforte duet (books iii., iv.) had been published simultaneously with the *Rhapsodies* by Simrock.
[^fn-p0132-1]: (*) Brahms betrays himself as a native of Hamburg in his use of *erinnern* for *sich erinnern*.
[^fn-p0133-1]: (*) Page 11, bar 7.
[^fn-p0135-1]: (*) No. 14 is Brahms’s original work, like several more of the dances (see Kalbeck, *Johannes Brahms*, i. 66).
[^fn-p0135-2]: (†) Brahms wrote his motet, Op. 74, No. 1, on words taken from Job. In writing the *Vier ernste Gesänge*, composed in 1895, Brahms may have recalled her words.
[^fn-p0135-3]: (‡) ‘*As the Hart pants*,’ Mendelssohn, Op. 42, No. 1.
[^fn-p0136-1]: (*) Op. 28. *Cf.* Letters 58, 62, 65.
[^fn-p0137-1]: (*) Brahms was twenty when he came to Schumann in 1853.
[^fn-p0137-2]: (†) A performance at the Hochschule on December 4, conducted by Joachim.
[^fn-p0137-3]: (‡) Brahms was not able to go to Berchtesgaden from Ischl until September 13, after the Herzogenbergs had left. Frau Schumann was staying at Vordereck, near Berchtesgaden, her summer house for some years, and Brahms had played to her and her family the *Academic* and *Tragic* overtures, and two movements from the C major trio, Op. 87, which he had written at Ischl. The ‘little girls’ were, of course, Marie Fillunger and Eugenie Schumann.
[^fn-p0138-1]: (*) Herzogenberg's *Sacred Volkslieder*.
[^fn-p0139-1]: (*) Joachim had played the violin concerto at the Gewandhaus, this being the third performance at Leipzig within the year.
[^fn-p0139-2]: (†) *Academische Festouvertüre*, p. 9 of full score, beginning at the ninth bar.
[^fn-p0139-3]: (‡) One of the Bach-Verein concerts.
[^fn-p0139-4]: (§) Cantata by Bach.
[^fn-p0140-1]: (*) Joachim had played his own variations for violin and orchestra from manuscript at the same concert as the Brahms violin concerto.
[^fn-p0140-2]: (†) Brahms was adapting some of Händel’s chamber duos for the Peters edition.
[^fn-p0140-3]: (‡) The elaborate contrapuntal movement which Brahms had evolved from the figured *Basso continuo*.
[^fn-p0140-4]: (§) Frau Emma Engelmann.
[^fn-p0140-5]: (‖) The *Academische Festouvertüre*, Op. 80.
[^fn-p0141-1]: (*) Christmas presents in Germany are spread out in separate piles, usually on separate tables, one for each member of the family. The time of presentation is the afternoon or evening of Christmas Eve ; it takes place in a room set apart for the occasion, of which the chief adornment is a lighted Christmas-tree. A hymn is often sung by way of preliminary, and the whole ceremony of entering the room and examining the presents is attended with much solemnity.—<span class="small-caps">Tr</span>.
[^fn-p0141-2]: (†) The *Academische Festouvertüre* had been written in recognition of the honorary doctor’s degree conferred on Brahms by the philosophical faculty of Breslau University. He conducted the first performance of this overture and its twin, the *Tragische*, on January 4, at the Breslau Orchestral Society.
[^fn-p0141-3]: (‡) Pp. 5-17 of full score.
[^fn-p0142-1]: (*) *Tragische Ouvertüre*, Op. 81.
[^fn-p0143-1]: (*) Brahms had written a canon on Uhland’s lines ‘ Wann hört der Himmel auf zu strafen Mit Albums und mit Autographen ?’ (When will this rain of albums cease, And autograph-hunters give us peace ?) and had given it to Naumann for his ‘ Illustrated History of Music.’
[^fn-p0143-2]: (†) Agnes Zimmermann (b. 1845), pianist and composer.
[^fn-p0144-1]: (*) First two lines of Heine’s *Loreley*.
[^fn-p0144-2]: (†) An allusion to Wagner’s weakness for satin.
[^fn-p0145-1]: (*) The desired autograph for Miss Mackenzie.
[^fn-p0145-2]: (†) Brahms had been at Breslau from January 1st to 7th, at Leipzig on the 13th, at Münster on the 22nd, at Krefeld on the 25th, at Amsterdam on the 31st, and in February at The Hague and at Haarlem.
[^fn-p0145-3]: (‡) The autograph took the form of a four-part canonic puzzle, which was published, according to Brahms’s suggestion, in No. 18 of the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, April 28, 1881. The riddle was solved the very next day by F. Böhme, and his solution was printed by Fritzsch on August 4, 1881. It is not commonly known to this day that Brahms was the composer.
[^fn-p0145-4]: (§) A joke on the name, Farren and Ziegen standing for bulls and goats respectively.—<span class="small-caps">Tr</span>.
[^fn-p0147-1]: (*) *I.e.*, thirty-two quarterings.—<span class="small-caps">Tr</span>.
[^fn-p0148-1]: (*) Brahms was studying Italian with a view to travelling in Italy.
[^fn-p0150-1]: (*) Brahms went to Italy on March 15. His second Italian tour was longer and more extended than the first. Theodor Billroth and Professor Adolf Exner travelled with him, but left him behind in Rome, where he wished to spend more time. The route lay through Venice, Florence, Siena, Orvieto, Rome, and Naples, to Sicily, returning by Florence and Pisa. Brahms returned to Vienna on May 7, his forty-eighth birthday.
[^fn-p0151-1]: (*) Wilhelm Rust (1822-1892), organist and cantor at St. Thomas’s.
[^fn-p0151-2]: (†) Schumann’s setting.
[^fn-p0151-3]: (‡) Bülow’s Beethoven performance (*cf.* Letter 84). The writer saw fit to correct her harsh judgment of Bülow later. Bülow only posed as a witty interpreter aiming at special effects—in his piano-playing as in his conducting—until he was sure of himself, his orchestra, and his audience. His extravagances were to him a necessary coercive measure. Once he felt himself master of the situation and at home with his audience, his interpretation lost its personal character, and became a simple expression of the music, which he had really at heart.
[^fn-p0153-1]: (*) Brahms, violin concerto :

    *OBOE.* [Musical notation] etc.
[^fn-p0154-1]: (*) Brahms was always reluctant to order a new suit of clothes on account of the trouble it involved. He would wear the same things year after year, and much resented being reminded by his friends that his wardrobe needed replenishing.
[^fn-p0154-2]: (†) Brahms used quill pens which he trimmed for himself.
[^fn-p0155-1]: (*) A fresh copy of the canon (*cf.* Letters 81 and 82) for the editor of the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*. Brahms’s solution was never published.
[^fn-p0155-2]: (†) This is not to be taken literally. He not only sent no descriptions of his travels, but declined to have letters forwarded.
[^fn-p0155-3]: (‡) An old-fashioned pleasure resort on the bank of the Saale.
[^fn-p0156-1]: (*) The letter is written in pencil.
[^fn-p0157-1]: (*) Friedrich Chrysander (1826-1901) edited Händel, and wrote his biography in the *Allgemeine Musikalische Zeitung*, of which he was editor. The article referred to is in No. 22 of vol. xvi.
[^fn-p0159-1]: (*) The letters had crossed.
[^fn-p0159-2]: (†) Two Halle doctors.
[^fn-p0160-1]: (*) The great B flat concerto, Op. 83, in four movements. Brahms sent it when completed to his friend Billroth on July 11, with the note : ‘I am sending you some small piano pieces.’ It was actually finished on July 7.
[^fn-p0160-2]: (†) *Scherzerl* is the name given to the crusty ends of a long roll of bread in Vienna.
[^fn-p0160-3]: (‡) Frau Herzogenberg’s own words in the original.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0161-1]: (*) From Schubert’s song, ‘The Wanderer.’—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0161-2]: (†) The melody of *Freut Euch des Lebens.*
[^fn-p0163-1]: (*) Her old master (*cf.* Letter 17, note).
[^fn-p0164-1]: (*) Brahms’s *Nänie* for chorus and orchestra, Op. 82, dedicated to the memory of Feuerbach, the artist, was begun in 1880, the year of his death. Brahms had completed it during the summer, and played it to the Herzogenbergs in Vienna.
[^fn-p0164-2]: (†) Overture by Ferdinand Hiller (1811-1885), pianist, composer, and writer on music.
[^fn-p0165-1]: (*) Symphonic poem for orchestra.
[^fn-p0165-2]: (†) Julius Röntgen.
[^fn-p0165-3]: (‡) Hiller had published a series of articles in the *Deutsche Rundschau*, called *Besuche im Jenseits*, which he afterwards incorporated in his *Erinnerungsblätter* (1884).
[^fn-p0165-4]: (§) Brahms had dedicated *Nänie* to Frau Henriette Feuerbach, the artist’s mother.
[^fn-p0166-1]: (*) Dr. Max Abraham, head of the firm of Peters at the time. *Nänie* was published by him in 1881.
[^fn-p0166-2]: (†) The two new compositions which Brahms had played to the Herzogenbergs in Vienna.
[^fn-p0166-3]: (‡) *Cf.* Letter 72.
[^fn-p0166-4]: (§) Ignaz Brüll (b. 1846), composer and pianist. Brahms much appreciated his quick comprehension and clever, musicianly playing, and often asked his assistance when he wished to introduce any of his larger compositions to his intimate friends in Vienna.
[^fn-p0167-1]: (*) See note to Letter 95.
[^fn-p0167-2]: (†) Oratorio.
[^fn-p0167-3]: (‡) The words are a quotation from *Nänie*.
[^fn-p0168-1]: (*) Bülow had surprised Brahms in July, 1881, with a very cordial invitation to come and use the perfectly drilled Ducal Orchestra (he had been *Hofmusikintendant* at Meiningen since October, 1880) to rehearse any novelties he might have. Brahms replied that he had only the piano concerto, which, much as it needed rehearsing, was hardly suitable for Meiningen. Bülow insisted to the contrary, and Brahms was easily persuaded. He went to Meiningen in the middle of October, was received with much consideration, and returned to play his concerto on November 27. Bülow conducted on this occasion, and the result was an ideal rendering. In the meantime Brahms had played it at Buda-Pesth on the 9th, and Hungary enjoys the honour of having secured the first public performance.
[^fn-p0169-1]: (*) The Committee decided on the piano concerto, which Brahms accordingly played on January 1st.
[^fn-p0171-1]: (*) Herzogenberg.
[^fn-p0172-1]: (*) The C minor symphony. See full score, p. 25, bars 13-15.
[^fn-p0172-2]: (†) The economy to be practised on the occasion of his own proposed visit to them.
[^fn-p0173-1]: (*) The Leipzig press notices. One of the critics was named Bernhard Vogel (=Bird), hence perhaps the expression. He wrote for the *Leipziger Neuesten Nachrichten*, and is the author of a monograph on Brahms. The *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, edited by Fritzsch, which had championed Brahms warmly from the first, was obliged to admit that the attitude of the public towards Brahms’s new compositions (he had played his concerto and the two rhapsodies, Op. 79, on New Year’s Day) was rather apathetic than encouraging. ‘One can hardly say,’ ran the notice, ‘that the *Gewandhäusler* showed any particular appreciation of their guest’s importance in general, or of his new work in particular.’
[^fn-p0174-1]: (*) Brahms had gone to Hamburg from Leipzig to play his new concerto there on January 6.
[^fn-p0174-2]: (†) The letter is missing.
[^fn-p0174-3]: (‡) The ‘ Brahms-concert ’ was the second of three concerts given at Leipzig by Bülow with the *Meininger Kapelle*. On this occasion he conducted the C minor symphony and the orchestral variations on a Haydn theme, and played the pianoforte concerto in D minor.
[^fn-p0175-1]: (*) Bülow’s object was to overcome what he considered to be an unwarranted prejudice against the works of Schumann’s latest period.
[^fn-p0175-2]: (†) Bülow.
[^fn-p0176-1]: (*) The third movement of the symphony.
[^fn-p0176-2]: (†) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0176-3]: (‡) Heinrich XXVI., Prince of Reuss-Köstritz (b. 1855), a well-known composer, pupil of Herzogenberg.
[^fn-p0176-4]: (§) Adolf Wach (b. 1843), a famous jurist, Mendelssohn’s son-in-law, had been Professor at Leipzig University since 1875.
[^fn-p0176-5]: (‖) Hedwig von Holstein.
[^fn-p0176-6]: (¶) Ernst von Stockhausen.
[^fn-p0178-1]: (*) In the C minor symphony.
[^fn-p0178-2]: (†) This is a mistake. The particular passage (p. 11, bar 2 of the full score) has this figure—

    [Musical notation] brought first by the violas, marked *col arco*, while the other strings give a *pizzicato* chord.
[^fn-p0179-1]: (*) P. 29 of the full score.
[^fn-p0179-2]: (†) Richard Mühlfeld, the famous clarinettist, for whom Brahms afterwards composed his various chamber pieces with clarinet.
[^fn-p0179-3]: (‡) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0179-4]: (§) Brahms had a particular affection for *mezzo-forte* effects.
[^fn-p0179-5]: (‖) The standard critical edition of Bach, published by Breitkopf and Härtel.
[^fn-p0180-1]: (*) Ernst von Stockhausen.
[^fn-p0180-2]: (†) *Kätchen von Heilbronn*, prize opera of the year (1881), by Karl Reinthaler,
[^fn-p0182-1]: (*) Brahms had responded to the ‘nice’ letters by sending a parcel of songs to form a supplement to those sent before, and now published as Op. 84-86.
[^fn-p0182-2]: (†) At the performance of the Requiem in Hamburg.
[^fn-p0182-3]: (‡) The close of the second part : ‘*Ewige Freude wird über ihrem Haupte sein.*’
[^fn-p0182-4]: (§) In the original the writer rings the changes on the four different meanings of the expression *am Ende*, using it to express ‘in the end,’ ‘after all,’ ‘on the whole,’ and ‘at the end,’ respectively.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0183-1]: (*) Op. 86, No. 1.
[^fn-p0184-1]: (*) She was to have gone there for another course of treatment for her heart trouble (*cf.* Letter 87).
[^fn-p0184-2]: (†) Julius Stockhausen had settled at Frankfurt to teach singing in 1878.
[^fn-p0184-3]: (‡) *Feldeinsamkeit*, Op. 86, No. 2.
[^fn-p0184-4]: (§) Epstein.
[^fn-p0185-1]: (*) The letter accompanied an autograph promised to Miss Ethel Smyth. Frau Herzogenberg’s letter containing the request is missing.
[^fn-p0185-2]: (†) Quotation from the song *Über die Heide*, Op. 86, No. 4, in which the last words actually read : *und der Himmel so leer*.
[^fn-p0185-3]: (‡) *Cf.* Letter 109, 110, the song quoted.
[^fn-p0185-4]: (§) Wagner’s *Parsifal* received its first performance at Bayreuth in July, 1882. The work was to be Bayreuth’s monopoly, as is well known. Brahms often regretted never having been there. In the summer of 1882 he writes to Bülow : ‘ The fact that I cannot come to a decision about Bayreuth probably means that I am unable to produce that “yes.” I need hardly say that I go in dread of the Wagnerians, who would spoil my pleasure in the best of Wagners. I don’t know yet what I shall do. I may take advantage of my beard, which still allows me to trot about so nice and anonymously.’
[^fn-p0186-1]: (*) Brahms’s summer home at Ischl in 1880 and 1882, and from 1889 to 1896 (*cf.* Victor von Miller’s *Brahmsbilderbuch*, pp. 98, 101.
[^fn-p0186-2]: (†) As Brahms had said in his letter that he must see the name Ethel Smyth printed on a title-page before he could be supposed to remember it, Frau von Herzogenberg jestingly assumed that he intended an indirect recommendation of the coming composer to publishers.
[^fn-p0187-1]: (*) A town in North-Western Bohemia.
[^fn-p0187-2]: (†) Schumann’s song-cycle.
[^fn-p0188-1]: (*) Otto Dessoff (1835-1892), formerly conductor of the Vienna Philharmonic, was appointed first conductor of the *Stadttheater* at Frankfurt in 1881.
[^fn-p0188-2]: (†) Dessoff’s little boy.
[^fn-p0189-1]: (*) The last of the three books of songs is numbered Op. 86.
[^fn-p0189-2]: (†) *Nachtwandler*, Op. 86, No. 3.
[^fn-p0189-3]: (‡) Op. 86, No. 2.
[^fn-p0190-1]: (*) Op. 86, No. 5.
[^fn-p0190-2]: (†) Op. 85, No. 6.
[^fn-p0190-3]: (‡) *Romanzen und Lieder*, Op. 84.
[^fn-p0190-4]: (§) Op. 84, No. 1.
[^fn-p0190-5]: (‖) C. W. Dietrich, painter and etcher of the eighteenth century.
[^fn-p0191-1]: (*) *In den Beeren*, Op. 84, No. 2.
[^fn-p0191-2]: (†) Op. 84, No. 5.
[^fn-p0191-3]: (‡) Brahms was learning Italian to arm himself for further travels in Italy.
[^fn-p0192-1]: (*) From Giuseppe Giusti’s *Raccolta di proverbi Toscani*, p. 126 (‘Words are [in Italian] of the feminine, deeds of the masculine gender’), or, more accurately, *Le parole son femmine e i fatti son maschi* (‘Words are women, deeds are men’).
[^fn-p0192-2]: (†) Arthur Faber, their mutual friend, was dangerously ill.
[^fn-p0192-3]: (‡) From Frau Schumann.
[^fn-p0192-4]: (§) Psalm cxvi. for mixed chorus *a capella*, Op. 34.
[^fn-p0193-1]: (*) The quintet in F, Op. 88 (MS.).
[^fn-p0193-2]: (†) Engelbert Röntgen, leader of the Gewandhaus orchestra and of the quartet.
[^fn-p0194-1]: (*) The passage reads as follows in the score, p. 22, bar 6 :

    [Musical notation]
[^fn-p0195-1]: (*) Neither this passage nor the previous one is accurately quoted (*cf.* p. 20 of the score, last four bars).
[^fn-p0195-2]: (†) See p. 11 of score. All the musical quotations scattered about the letters are from memory, the manuscript having been returned before these notes were made.
[^fn-p0196-1]: (*) P. 6 of score.
[^fn-p0196-3]: (†) P. 9, bar 3.
[^fn-p0196-2]: (‡) P. 11, bar 6.

[^fn-p0197-2]: (†) P. 12, bar 10.
[^fn-p0197-3]: (‡) P. 13, bar 10.
[^fn-p0197-4]: (§) P. 12, bar 7.
[^fn-p0197-5]: (‖) P. 19, bars 5 and 6.
[^fn-p0198-1]: (*) Quotation from the *Nachwandler*, Op. 86.
[^fn-p0198-2]: (†) The resemblance was purely accidental, but Brahms was, as a matter of fact, fond of appropriating other people’s themes, partly as a challenge to the critics, whose comments on their discoveries always amused him.
[^fn-p0199-1]: (*) *Cf.* Letter 65.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0199-2]: (†) The estate of the Faber family.
[^fn-p0200-1]: (*) Brahms alludes to the liberty he had taken in *O schöne Nacht* (*cf.* Letters 15 and 19).
[^fn-p0200-2]: (†) Brahms really prided himself on his Biblical knowledge, in which he was a match for any theologian. He had always taken pleasure in hunting up ‘godless’ texts in the Bible. Nothing made him angrier than to be taken for an orthodox Church composer on account of his sacred compositions. His *Vier ernste Gesänge* (see Letters 275 and 277) are not the only protests he made.
[^fn-p0200-3]: (‡) The note-paper bore a vignette of Bülow, who had been married to Marie Schanzer in July.
[^fn-p0201-1]: (*) Of the Quintet in F.
[^fn-p0202-1]: (*) The Concerto in B flat, in Brahms’s own arrangement for two pianos, was published by Simrock at the end of the year 1882.
[^fn-p0202-2]: (†) Probably Professor Engelmann from Utrecht.
[^fn-p0202-3]: (‡) A *Tonkünstlerversammlung* of the *Allgemeinen deutschen Musikverein* was held at Leipzig from May 3 to May 6. Karl Riedel, one of the founders, had been president of the *Verein* since Brendel’s death in 1868.
[^fn-p0202-4]: (§) A string quartet by Rimsky-Korsakoff, and Borodine’s Symphony in E flat.
[^fn-p0203-1]: (*) *Song of the Fates* for chorus and orchestra, Op. 89.
[^fn-p0203-2]: (†) Franz Liszt, who had been specially invited to the Festival.
[^fn-p0203-3]: (‡) Arthur Nikisch (b. 1855), the famous conductor, at that time first conductor of the Leipzig Opera.
[^fn-p0204-1]: (*) Probably Chodowiecki’s *Travels, Berlin to Danzig, in* 1873, a facsimile reproduction.
[^fn-p0204-2]: (†) Dr. Adolf Brodsky, violinist, at that time Professor at Leipzig Conservatorium, now Principal of the Royal College of Music, Manchester.
[^fn-p0204-3]: (‡) Op. 85, No. 6.
[^fn-p0204-4]: (§) A wooded park just outside the town.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0205-1]: (*) Brahms’s way of sending an autograph.
[^fn-p0205-2]: (†) Eugen d’Albert (b. 1864), famous pianist and composer.
[^fn-p0205-3]: (‡) The opening of Liszt’s Concerto in E flat, which D’Albert played on the second day of the Festival.
[^fn-p0205-4]: (§) A Viennese street-song.
[^fn-p0205-5]: (‖) Brahms played his B flat concerto and conducted his second symphony there at the Sixtieth Lower Rhine Festival (May 11-15).
[^fn-p0206-1]: (*) Brahms finished his third symphony here in the course of the summer.
[^fn-p0206-2]: (†) Joachim Raff (1822-1882), composer. The *Tageszeiten* (Op. 209) is a cantata in four movements for chorus, piano, and orchestra.
[^fn-p0207-1]: (*) Quotation from *Parsifal* (*Zum Raum wird hier die Zeit*).
[^fn-p0207-2]: (†) Brahms’s opponents had diligently spread the rumour of his leaving Vienna, which had arisen from a casual remark of his to the effect that it was becoming practically impossible for a German to live in Vienna. Count Taaffe’s Czech policy was abhorrent to him.
[^fn-p0207-3]: (‡) Hermann Wolff (1845-1902), the first concert agent, known as ‘Concert Wolff.’
[^fn-p0208-1]: (*) Ludwig Meinardus (1827 - 1896), writer and composer. His oratorio *Luther at Worms* had a considerable vogue on the occasion of Luther’s Fourth Centenary, celebrated throughout Protestant Germany on November 10, 1883.
[^fn-p0208-2]: (†) Bach’s Reformation cantata.
[^fn-p0208-3]: (‡) The Wagner quotation in Letter 123.
[^fn-p0209-1]: (*) In Berlin. Joachim conducted it on January 4, 1884, while Brahms conducted it himself at Wüllner’s concert shortly after,
[^fn-p0210-1]: (*) Quartet in A minor, Op. 51, No. 2.
[^fn-p0210-2]: (†) Symphony No. 1.
[^fn-p0210-3]: (‡) Sillem and Koopmann by name, friends of Brahms from Amsterdam, who were at Leipzig on a visit.
[^fn-p0210-4]: (§) From the ’cello sonata, Op. 36.
[^fn-p0211-1]: (*) A deliberate use of ‘when’ for ‘if’ as a specimen of South German dialect.
[^fn-p0211-2]: (†) Of the firm Peters.
[^fn-p0211-3]: (‡) Miss Ethel Smyth.
[^fn-p0211-4]: (§) Henri Petri (b. 1856), pupil of Joachim and of Brussels Conservatoire, had been appointed leader of the Gewandhaus orchestra in 1882.
[^fn-p0211-5]: (‖) The ‘if’ and ‘when’ are a protest against the ‘when’ in the preceding letter.
[^fn-p0212-1]: (*) ‘Modest’—in reference to a quibble he had once had over the word with Frau von Herzogenberg, and also with a view to damping her expectations.
[^fn-p0212-2]: (†) Duets for soprano and tenor, with pianoforte accompaniment, Op. 38. Brahms specifies No. 1, *Die Waise* ; No. 2, *Begegnung* ; No. 8, *Äolsharfen* ; No. 9, *Im Abendrot*. Herzogenberg named the new house he built for his wife at Heiden, on Lake Constance, after the last of these. She died before it was finished,
[^fn-p0213-1]: (*) For the purpose of publication.  They were the six *Lieder und Romanzen für vierstimmigen gemischten Chor, a capella*, Op. 93*a*.
[^fn-p0213-2]: (†) Georg Muffat (1645-1704), famous in his day as an organ composer.  The *Passacaglia* in G was from his *Apparatus Musico-organisticus*.  Brahms admired it very much, and had copied it out for his own use.
[^fn-p0213-3]: (‡) Bizet’s *Carmen*, for which Brahms had at first conceived a violent dislike, on account of its unconcerned mingling of tragedy and light comedy.  But the French composer’s wealth of melody, and his broad treatment of his subject, soon triumphed over Brahms’s æsthetic scruples, and the consciousness of having been unjust (if only in thought) to an inspired musician helped to drive him to the other extreme.  He never wavered afterwards in his love for *Carmen*, which even usurped the place of *Die weisse Dame* in his affections at times.
[^fn-p0214-1]: (*) *Lieder und Romanzen*, Op. 93*a*, Nos. 4, 3, 2.
[^fn-p0214-2]: (†) Herzogenberg’s string quartet was published, together with the two earlier ones, by Rieter-Biedermann as Op. 42. The three are dedicated to *seinem hochverehrten Freunde* Johannes Brahms.
[^fn-p0215-1]: (*) Her æsthetic instinct was not at fault, but she was too open-minded to reject the good with the bad.
[^fn-p0215-2]: (†) See Letter 103, note.
[^fn-p0215-3]: (‡) See Letter 127.
[^fn-p0216-1]: (*) An old collection of German volkslieder.
[^fn-p0216-2]: (†) Böhme’s *Altdeutsches Liederbuch* (*cf.* Letter 62, note, and Kalbeck, *Life of Brahms*, i. 390).
[^fn-p0216-3]: (‡) After the first performance of this symphony (No. 3 in F) in Vienna on December 2, 1883, a veritable triumph for the composer, various daily papers and periodicals had asserted that not only did it far outshine its predecessors, but also that it was the best thing Brahms had ever produced. Brahms was exceedingly annoyed by this extravagant and unjust praise, especially as it raised expectations which he thought could not be fulfilled. He conducted the symphony at Wiesbaden, where it had been written, on January 18, 1884.
[^fn-p0217-1]: (*) Professor Engelmann had obtained a long leave on account of his health.
[^fn-p0218-1]: (*) Brahms had conducted the symphony on February 7 at the Gewandhaus, after which Hermine Spiess sang Schubert’s *Memnon* and *Geheimes* to the orchestral accompaniment arranged by Brahms, and several Brahms songs. On the day before, Brahms played his violin sonata at the first concert of the new Brodsky quartet.
[^fn-p0218-2]: (†) Brahms conducted the symphony and the *Song of the Fates* at the eighth Gürzenich concert on February 12.
[^fn-p0218-3]: (‡) The romantic horn part, p. 24 of the score, bar 5.
[^fn-p0219-1]: (*) The reference is to the three string quartets mentioned in Letter 128.
[^fn-p0220-1]: (*) A succession of concerts devoted to Brahms’s music was given in Dresden, March 5-10. On the 5th Brahms conducted his *Rhapsodie* (Hermine Spiess being the soloist), and the final chorus from *Rinaldo*; on the 7th his new symphony (No. 3); while Bülow played the Pianoforte Sonata in F minor on the 10th. Frau Herzogenberg’s serious illness prevented their going.
[^fn-p0221-1]: (*) The cantatas, written out by a contemporary copyist, had been presented to Brahms by Eduard Hanslick, who had received them from an admirer, Armin Friedmann, who in his turn had them from an antiquarian in Leipzig. Hanslick was just going to Karlsbad for his health, and left them with Brahms. Brahms’s letter in reply was of extraordinary length for him, and was obviously intended to be made public. It is typical of his attitude towards the fashion of printing everything bearing a distinguished name, and has been included in Hanslick’s musical essays, published in 1899 under the title *Am Ende des Jahrhunderts* (see pp. 379-383).
[^fn-p0222-1]: (*) Educated men are usually able to avoid the compulsory three years’ service by serving one year at their own expense.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0222-2]: (†) Chrysander’s Händel biography, of which the first half of the third volume had appeared in 1867, remained unfinished.
[^fn-p0222-3]: (‡) Motet by Brahms, Op. 74, No. 1 (Job iii. 20).
[^fn-p0223-1]: (*) Brahms had in mind principally the house of Rudolf and Laura Beckerath, where he was quite at home.
[^fn-p0224-1]: (*) Anton Door (b. 1833), Viennese pianist, whose trio-evenings were very popular.
[^fn-p0224-2]: (†) The post of Director of the Gürzenich concerts and the Conservatorium was to become vacant in October through the retirement of Ferdinand Hiller (1811-1884), and there were negotiations on foot to appoint Brahms successor.
[^fn-p0224-3]: (‡) Brahms had evidently forgotten his letter on the subject, written on March 28.
[^fn-p0225-1]: (*) Brahms had refused the post.
[^fn-p0226-1]: (*) Symphony in F.
[^fn-p0226-2]: (†) Written at the Wachs’ house.
[^fn-p0226-3]: (‡) A reproduction of Wilhelm Hensel’s pencil drawing. Brahms liked the serious, expressive face of the child, and hung up the portrait in his music-room.
[^fn-p0227-1]: (*) His birthday.
[^fn-p0227-2]: (†) Brahms’s fourth tour in Italy was made in company with Rudol v. d. Leyen, a friend from Krefeld. They went by way of Trient and Lake Garda to Upper Italy, where they were the guests of the Duke of Meiningen at Villa Carlotta on Lake Como (see R. v. d. Leyen’s *Johannes Brahms als Mensch und Freund*, p. 40).
[^fn-p0228-1]: (*) Brahms conducted his third symphony and the *Song of the Fates* at the Lower Rhine Festival.
[^fn-p0228-2]: (†) Richard Barth (b. 1850), violinist and conductor; author of a valuable monograph, *Johannes Brahms und seine Musik*.
[^fn-p0228-3]: (‡) The Herzogenbergs’ new address.
[^fn-p0229-1]: (*) Herzogenberg had been offered the post of Principal of the *Academische Meisterschule* for composition, and director of the theoretical side at the *Hochschule*, together with the title of Professor.
[^fn-p0229-2]: (†) Friedrich Kiel (1821-1885), composer and theorist, Herzogenberg’s predecessor at the *Hochschule*.
[^fn-p0229-3]: (‡) Bach’s cantata.
[^fn-p0230-1]: (*) Brahms’s songs from Tieck’s *Magelone*, Op. 33, and the *Serenaden*, Op. 11 and 16.
[^fn-p0231-1]: (*) The paragraph suppressed in the preceding letter contained an eloquent description of a poor musician in great straits through no fault of his own.
[^fn-p0231-2]: (†) The manuscript of *Gestillte Sehnsucht*, Op. 91, No. 1.
[^fn-p0231-3]: (‡) In Emil Breslaur’s music-teachers’ periodical *Der Klavierlehrer*. Brahms was fond of using *mp* to denote a fine shade between ‘mezzo-forte’ and ‘piano.’
[^fn-p0231-4]: (§) *Musikalisches Wochenblatt.*
[^fn-p0232-1]: (*) The fund which was being collected for a musician.
[^fn-p0232-2]: (†) Refers to the same fund.
[^fn-p0233-1]: (*) Poem by Schiller, composed by Schubert.
[^fn-p0233-2]: (†) A piano with tuned forks in place of strings, invented by Fritzsch and Fischer.
[^fn-p0233-3]: (‡) Op. 91, No. 1.
[^fn-p0233-4]: (§) Beethoven.
[^fn-p0233-5]: (‖) The second of the two songs, with viola obligato, published in 1884 as Op. 91.
[^fn-p0233-6]: (¶) The fourth symphony was commenced in the summer of 1884 at Mürzzuschlag.
[^fn-p0234-1]: (*) Brahms was never known to discuss his plans or his compositions while they were in progress, but this did not prevent the wildest guesses and conclusions on the part of others. Röntgen could only have learned the fact by the merest chance.
[^fn-p0234-2]: (†) Copies of the Bach cantatas, written out in Brahms’s own writing, had been offered for sale.
[^fn-p0235-1]: (*) Herzogenberg had ordered a whole year of the periodical through Fritzsch (see Letter 141).
[^fn-p0235-2]: (†) J. van Santen Kolff, a contributor to the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, who had written a series of essays on *Erinnerungsmotiv-Leitmotiv*.
[^fn-p0235-3]: (‡) In G minor, Op. 42, dedicated to Brahms. Robert Heckmann’s quartet evenings soon became a popular institution.
[^fn-p0235-4]: (§) Bülow’s tour with the Meininger orchestra in November and December included concerts in Vienna, Buda-Pesth, and Graz, at which Brahms played his B flat major concerto.
[^fn-p0235-5]: (‖) Towards the fund mentioned in Letter 150.
[^fn-p0236-1]: (*) To inaugurate the opening of the new Gewandhaus on December 11, 12, and 13.
[^fn-p0236-2]: (†) Robert Franz’s edition of the *Messiah*.
[^fn-p0237-1]: (*) One of the many false reports spread about Brahms’s work.
[^fn-p0238-1]: (*) Ottokar Novaček (b. 1866), violinist and composer.
[^fn-p0239-1]: (*) Brahms was very fond of this wine, which reminded him of Sicily.
[^fn-p0239-2]: (†) Ferdinand Bischof, to whom Herzogenberg dedicated his *Vier Gesänge*, Op. 40.
[^fn-p0240-1]: (*) Anton Bruckner (1827-1896), a much-fêted Austrian composer, whose works gave rise to considerable controversy. Nikisch was conducting his seventh symphony at a ‘special’ concert at the opera-house.
[^fn-p0240-2]: (†) Besides the rare Czerny edition of Scarlatti, Brahms owned several of the sonatas in the original edition, and over 300 old copies of the *Klavierstücke*, amongst them 172 unpublished pieces from the famous collection of Abbate Santini.
[^fn-p0240-3]: (‡) Brahms had undertaken the revision of Schubert’s symphonies for the complete edition, published by Breitkopf and Härtel, a responsibility he afterwards regretted. His objections to the posthumous publication of inferior compositions, which the composer is no longer able to defend, are expressed with great plainness in a letter to Marie Lipsius (La Mara), written in May, 1885 (see La Mara, *Musikerbriefe aus fünf Jahrhunderten*, ii. 348). But when the ten volumes of Schubert’s songs appeared (edited by Mandyczewski), he changed his mind, and regretted the superficial way in which he had fulfilled his share of the work, as much as his declaration that there was no sense in publishing these examples of an early stage of the composer’s development.
[^fn-p0241-1]: (*) See Letter 130, note.
[^fn-p0242-1]: (*) Brahms, Op. 46, No. 1 ; Op. 47, Nos. 1, 5 ; Op. 49, No. 5.
[^fn-p0242-2]: (†) Ludwig Theodor Gouvy (1822-1898), distinguished composer.
[^fn-p0243-1]: (*) Johannes Wolf (b. 1869), a pupil of Spitta, subsequently gave up composing for music history, and became tutor at Berlin University.
[^fn-p0243-2]: (†) ‘*Der Tod, das ist die kühle Nacht*,’ Op. 96, No. 1.
[^fn-p0244-1]: (*) Paul Klengel (1854), a violinist, elder brother of Julius ; conductor of the ‘ Euterpe ’ at Leipzig from 1881 to 1886.
[^fn-p0244-2]: (†) Founded on an old sacred melody, ‘*Josef, lieber Josef mein,*’ which Brahms found in Corner’s *Gross-Katholisches Gesangbuch* of 1631. Liszt made it a leading theme in his *St. Elisabeth*, and Herzogenberg in his *Geburt Christi*.
[^fn-p0244-3]: (‡) Hermine Spies, the singer.
[^fn-p0245-1]: (*) The *Vierteljahrsschrift für Musikwissenschaft* had just been started by Spitta, Chrysander, and Adler. It was published by Breitkopf and Härtel up to 1894.
[^fn-p0245-2]: (†) Chrysander lived at Bergedorf, near Hamburg.
[^fn-p0245-3]: (‡) Probably refers to Herzogenberg’s symphony in C minor, Op. 50, then in progress.

[^fn-p0246-1]: (*) The fourth symphony in E minor.
[^fn-p0246-2]: (†) Letter 150.
[^fn-p0246-3]: (‡) The third symphony in D minor, dedicated to Richard Wagner, and the quintet in F major.
[^fn-p0247-1]: (*) A new translation of Sophocles by Gustav Wendt (b. 1827), dedicated to Brahms, and presented by him to Frau von Herzogenberg.
[^fn-p0248-1]: (*) Josef Jakob Christian Donner, the well-known translator of the classics.
[^fn-p0248-2]: (†) Christoph Theodor Schwab, the first editor of Hölderlin’s complete works.
[^fn-p0248-3]: (‡) The Leipzig *Bachverein* performed the St. John Passion-music in celebration of Bach’s birthday.
[^fn-p0248-4]: (§) Cherubini’s opera.
[^fn-p0248-5]: (‖) Brahms had written a *Tafellied* (table-song), ‘*Dank der Damen*,’ Op. 93*b*, for the fiftieth anniversary of the Krefeld *Singverein* on January 27 and 28, and conducted it himself.
[^fn-p0249-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s appointment to the *Hochschule*.
[^fn-p0249-2]: (†) Herzogenberg had, further, been elected member of the Academy of Arts. A portrait included in this volume shows him in his academical robes.
[^fn-p0250-1]: (*) Op. 54 ; quotation from the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*.
[^fn-p0250-2]: (†) Bruckner’s quintet was performed in Vienna for the first time on January 8, 1885, at the Hellmesberger quartet concert. The Adagio alone had a *succès d’estime*.
[^fn-p0250-3]: (‡) Wilhelm Kupfer, a ’cellist, native of Hamburg, acted for many years as copyist to Brahms.
[^fn-p0250-4]: (§) Eduard Strauss’s orchestra.
[^fn-p0251-1]: (*) Op. 97, No. 1, and Op. 106, No. 5.
[^fn-p0251-2]: (†) Brahms had taken the melody to ‘*Hier, wo sich die Strassen scheiden*’ from the *Wanderer*, and adapted it for the *Nightingale*. Any attempt, therefore, to detect the nightingale’s note in the four introductory bars or the song itself is obviously unjustifiable. There could be no better argument against ‘ programme music.’
[^fn-p0251-3]: (‡) *Cf.* Letter 149.
[^fn-p0252-1]: (*) Brahms’s summer house.
[^fn-p0252-2]: (†) ‘*Wir Wandelten*,’ Op. 96, No. 2.
[^fn-p0253-1]: (*) Op. 96, No. 2.
[^fn-p0253-2]: (†) Bars 3, 29, 58.
[^fn-p0253-3]: (‡) Bar 48 *et seq.*
[^fn-p0253-4]: (§) Bar 54.
[^fn-p0254-1]: (*) ‘*Nein, trauter Vogel, nein !*’
[^fn-p0254-2]: (†) Quotation from Goethe’s *Gefunden*.
[^fn-p0254-3]: (‡) As a matter of fact, the *Wanderer* was the older of the twin songs, and had therefore the greater claim to being ‘*Gefunden*.’ Brahms may have had the *Nightingale* in his mind, but it was not written until later.
[^fn-p0254-4]: (§) Brahms eventually recast the *Wanderer*, and included it in Op. 106 much later.
[^fn-p0254-5]: (‖) Heine’s poem ‘*Wie der Mond sich leuchtend dränget*.’ Brahms suppressed the song, possibly on account of this adverse criticism, and gave up the idea of a series of Heine songs which he was then contemplating.
[^fn-p0255-1]: (*) ‘*Es schauen die Blumen*,’ Op. 96, No. 3.
[^fn-p0255-2]: (†) Op. 97, No. 2.
[^fn-p0255-3]: (‡) ‘*Trennung*,’ Op. 97, No. 6. The key is F, not E flat. Brahms transposed the song a semitone, probably to make it more effective. The unpleasant false relation in bar 14 is avoided as follows :

    [Musical notation]
[^fn-p0256-1]: (*) This song was never published. There is no *Winter’s Night* among Halm’s poems, but a *Snowstorm*, which corresponds to the description.
[^fn-p0256-2]: (†) ‘*Entführung*,’ Op. 97, No. 3, words by W. Alexis.
[^fn-p0256-3]: (‡) Brahms followed this advice, and lengthened the last verse by one bar to lend the climax additional emphasis.
[^fn-p0257-1]: (*) Princess Marie of Meiningen.
[^fn-p0258-1]: (*) An adaptation of Heine in ‘*Es schauen die Blumen*’ (Brahms, Op. 96, No. 3).
[^fn-p0259-1]: (*) Op. 97, No. 4.
[^fn-p0260-1]: (*) *Weidenlied*, Op. 97, No. 4. The melody is quoted correctly, but not the harmonies of the ritornelle. Brahms uses the dominant seventh on A, not the diminished seventh on A sharp, in the third bar.
[^fn-p0260-2]: (†) *Cf.* Kalbeck (Johannes Brahms, i. 160 *et seq.*).
[^fn-p0260-3]: (‡) ‘Sapphic Ode,’ Op. 94, No. 4.
[^fn-p0261-1]: (*) Op. 97, No. 3 (see Letter 158, note).
[^fn-p0261-2]: (†) Brahms had no such objection, but frequently handled his poems with great freedom.
[^fn-p0261-3]: (‡) *Meerfahrt.*
[^fn-p0261-4]: (§) Frau von Herzogenberg rightly traces the motif in the *Nightingale* and the *Wanderer* to the blackbird’s note, and not the nightingale’s.
[^fn-p0262-1]: (*) The name given to the house Herzogenberg had built at Berchtesgaden.
[^fn-p0262-2]: (†) George II., Duke of Saxe-Meiningen and Hildburghausen (b. 1826), was an intelligent patron of music and the drama. He married in 1873 a very musical actress, Ellen Franz, a pupil of Bülow, who received the title *Freifrau* von Heldburg. From the time of Bülow’s appointment as conductor at Meiningen in 1880, the Duke took a great interest in Brahms, whose frequent visits to the palace ended in establishing particularly friendly relations between them. Meiningen was the first town to erect a monument to Brahms. This was executed by Hildebrand a year after the composer’s death in 1897.
[^fn-p0263-1]: (*) Quotation from Hoffmann.
[^fn-p0263-2]: (†) At Vordereck, near Berchtesgaden.
[^fn-p0263-3]: (‡) The Duke had a hunting-lodge on Königssee.
[^fn-p0264-1]: (*) First movement of the fourth symphony in E minor.
[^fn-p0264-2]: (†) Brahms also considered it necessary to warn his friend Bülow of the acerbity of his new symphony. He says, writing about this time : ‘ I have often, while writing, had a pleasing vision of rehearsing it [the symphony] with you in a nice leisurely way—a vision that I still have, although I wonder if it will ever have any other audience ! I rather fear it has been influenced by this climate, where the cherries never ripen. You would never touch them !’
[^fn-p0264-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg’s first symphony in C minor, Op. 50, which was followed later by one other in B flat, Op. 70.
[^fn-p0264-4]: (§) Herzogenberg’s publisher.
[^fn-p0265-1]: (*) ‘Boys, brought forth in midnights haunted’ (*Faust II.*, Act V., Scene 7).
[^fn-p0265-2]: (†) Confuses ‘Dr. Marianus’ with ‘Pater Seraphicus’ in *Faust*.
[^fn-p0266-1]: (*) The symphony.
[^fn-p0266-2]: (†) The first movement of the fourth symphony in E minor; also the beginning of the *andante* written on the last page.
[^fn-p0267-1]: (*) Brahms had enclosed manuscript copies of the two songs which had particularly taken her fancy (Op. 96, Nos. 2 and 4).
[^fn-p0267-2]: (†) Moriz v. Kaiserfeld, son of the Austrian politician and former Governor of the Province of Steiermark, was devoted to music, and a great admirer of Brahms. When Brahms was about to try over his new quintet in F at Ladislaus von Wagner’s house at Alt-Aussee (August 19, 1882), it was found that no second viola-player was forthcoming ; whereupon Kaiserfeld, who, though a violinist, had never had a viola in his hand, was persuaded to undertake the part. He acquitted himself so well that Brahms copied into his album the viola theme from the first movement, with the remark : ‘ First viola, indifferent ; second viola, entirely satisfactory.’
[^fn-p0268-1]: (*) See p. 4 of the score, last bar.
[^fn-p0268-2]: (†) P. 7 of the score, first and following bars.

[^fn-p0269-1]: (*) As Brahms had not heard again from Frau von Herzogenberg, he concluded that her earlier remarks had only been a cloak for her embarrassment, and that the symphony had failed to please either the Herzogenbergs or Frau Schumann.
[^fn-p0269-2]: (†) See the addition to this letter, dated September 8.
[^fn-p0269-3]: (‡) ‘In their loves and hatreds there is sometimes reason ; never in their judgments and opinions’ (Goethe).
[^fn-p0270-1]: (*) Hanslick had at first the same impression. When Brahms played it with Ignaz Brüll on two pianos to a few of his friends in Vienna, Hanslick, who was present, sighed heavily after the first movement, and remarked: ‘Really, you know, it sounds to me like two tremendously witty people quarrelling.’
[^fn-p0270-2]: (†) P. 20 of the score, bar 5.
[^fn-p0271-1]: (*) The first G sharp in the third bar is a slip of the pen, or a case of defective memory. It should be G.

[^fn-p0272-5]: (‖) P. 30, bar 12. The various inaccuracies are all due to quoting from memory.
[^fn-p0273-1]: (*) A common German proverb, perhaps the nearest equivalent to ‘C’est le premier pas qui coûte.’—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0274-1]: (*) Op. 96, Nos. 2 and 4.
[^fn-p0277-1]: (*) See score, p. 5, bar 11.

[^fn-p0278-1]: (*) Brahms had arranged the symphony as a duet for two pianos.
[^fn-p0278-2]: (†) The movement is in the form of a strict *passacaglia*, with the eight-bars theme varied in thirty different ways. Brahms himself and others to whom he had shown the movement were afraid it might prove too monotonous as a Finale.
[^fn-p0279-1]: (*) Dr. Richter had announced it by way of a novelty at the Vienna Philharmonic.
[^fn-p0279-2]: (†) Brahms had been feeling very subdued in consequence of the lukewarm reception his new and very inaccessible symphony had met with from his intimate friends at a private performance, and was prepared to put it aside altogether should it fail to please at the Meiningen rehearsal.
[^fn-p0279-3]: (‡) These two lines are written slanting across the inside of the sheet in the original.
[^fn-p0279-4]: (§) Brahms, whose depression had not been dispelled by the rehearsal of his symphony at Meiningen, was again upset by receiving no acknowledgment of the music he had sent Frau von Herzogenberg. He therefore demanded the duet back, and indulged in a little sarcasm about her expected letter which had never arrived. The whole of his letter bears evidence to his irritability at the time.
[^fn-p0280-1]: (*) See Letter 169.
[^fn-p0280-3]: (†) Her father, mother, and sister.
[^fn-p0280-2]: (‡) See Letter 171.
[^fn-p0280-4]: (§) Meaning her family.
[^fn-p0281-1]: (*) When Mozart was at Leipzig in 1789, he heard a performance of Bach’s cantata *Sing unto the Lord a New Song*, by the *Thomaner*; and on learning that there was a whole collection of Bach’s cantatas there, but no complete scores, he had the written parts brought to him, spread them all around him, and was not to be moved from the spot until he read them all through (see Rochlitz in the *Allgemeine Musikalische Zeitung*, 1799, No. 8).
[^fn-p0282-1]: (*) Refers to Fritzsch, whose paper, the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, strongly championed Wagner.
[^fn-p0282-2]: (†) Score of the E minor symphony, p. 20, bar 8.
[^fn-p0282-3]: (‡) P. 5, bar 12.

There are no trombones in the first three movements. They are reserved for the *finale*, where they bring in the theme of the *passacaglia* with such a shattering effect.

[^fn-p0294-1]: (*) At Frankfurt on November 3.

[^fn-p0295-1]: (*) To Holland, via Essen and Elberfeld, for a series of concerts in Amsterdam, The Hague, and Utrecht.
[^fn-p0296-1]: (*) Julius Prüwer is described in Theodor Helm’s *Kalender für die musikalische Welt* as ‘prodigy,’ ‘pianist,’ and (1894) ‘concert pianist’ successively. He is now conductor of the Breslau Opera.
[^fn-p0296-2]: (†) Hans Schmitt (b. 1835), pianist and composer, professor at the Vienna Conservatoire, and author of various works on teaching.
[^fn-p0296-3]: (‡) A concert-hall in Vienna, belonging to the piano-manufacturer Ludwig Bösendorfer.
[^fn-p0296-4]: (§) Heinrich Barth (b. 1847), pupil of Bülow, Bronsart, and Tausig, a professor at the *Hochschule*, Berlin.

[^fn-p0300-2]: (†) The famous historical recitals, comprising piano music of all periods in chronological order.
[^fn-p0301-1]: (*) Her father, who died suddenly on December 29. This brief expression of sympathy was written on a post-card, which is a proof of Brahms’s indifference to convention.
[^fn-p0301-2]: (†) Joachim conducted the E minor symphony on February 1 at the Philharmonic concert, and deemed it wise to call the last movement *Variations*, the theme of the *passacaglia* being printed on the programmes.

[^fn-p0304-2]: (†) Woldemar Bargiel (1828-1897), composer, stepbrother of Frau Schumann’s.
[^fn-p0304-3]: (‡) See score, pp. 90 and 88.
[^fn-p0305-1]: (*) Otto Gumprecht (1823-1900), music critic of the *Nationalzeitung*, known as the ‘Berlin Hanslick.’
[^fn-p0305-2]: (†) Frau Röntgen.

[^fn-p0306-1]: (*) Brahms’s collection of autographs included nearly thirty loose pages and the sketch-book to the sonata, Op. 106, in Beethoven’s own hand.
[^fn-p0306-2]: (†) Gräfin Wilhelmine von Wickenburg-Almasy (b. 1845), poetess.
[^fn-p0307-1]: (*) Brahms conducted his E minor symphony at the Gewandhaus on February 18. Brodsky played the violin concerto at the same concert.
[^fn-p0307-2]: (†) ‘O Tannenbaum, O Tannenbaum, wie grün sind deine Blätter’ (popular song).—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0308-1]: (*) The manuscript of Schubert’s song *Am Grabe Anselmos*.
[^fn-p0309-1]: (*) See score of violin concerto, p. 60, bar 3.
[^fn-p0310-1]: (*) The Schubert and Beethoven manuscripts (*cf.* Letter 186).
[^fn-p0311-1]: (*) Besides the song *Am Grabe Anselmos*, Frau von Herzogenberg had presented to Brahms a set of Schubert's *Ländlers*.
[^fn-p0311-2]: (†) Brahms conducted his E minor symphony in Dresden on March 10.
[^fn-p0313-1]: (*) Probably the Finale of Herzogenberg’s ’cello sonata, Op. 52, which is dedicated to Hausmann.
[^fn-p0313-2]: (†) The Schumann symphony (*cf.* Letter 189).
[^fn-p0314-1]: (*) Brahms’s sonata for violoncello in F, Op. 99.
[^fn-p0314-2]: (†) Sonata for violin and piano in A, Op. 100, composed at Thun in the summer of 1886, as also the ’cello sonata. By Intermezzo the Andante is probably meant.
[^fn-p0314-3]: (‡) The Schumann symphony.
[^fn-p0315-1]: (*) Professor Dr. Eusebius Mandyczewski (b. 1857), composer, writer, editor of Schubert’s works, librarian of the Vienna *Gesellschaft der Musikfreunde*, was, during the last ten years of Brahms’s life, his untiring amanuensis and most faithful musical adviser.
[^fn-p0315-2]: (†) Frau von Herzogenberg’s correspondence with Brahms had ceased abruptly in March, 1886, on account of a report spread by some busybody, which afterwards proved to be quite unfounded. Brahms either ignored the fact or pretended to do so. He sent her the ’cello sonata, however, unaccompanied by any sort of message.
[^fn-p0315-3]: (‡) The painter Hans Makart, who was famous for his taciturnity, was once at a dinner-party, when his neighbour, Josephine Gallmeyer, after one of his long silences, turned on him with, ‘And now let us change the subject, dear Herr Makart.’
[^fn-p0316-1]: (*) Brahms often accompanied his playing by uncouth noises, which were sometimes so loud as to be audible to a concert audience.
[^fn-p0317-1]: (*) Brahms had not given Hermine Spies permission to sing in public the two songs, composed at Thun in the summer of 1886, of which he had sent her copies—*Wie Melodien zieht es* and *Immer leiser wird mein Schlummer* (Op. 105, Nos. 1 and 2)—but Frau von Herzogenberg had been allowed to see them (*cf.* Hermine Spies, *Ein Gedenkbuch*, p. 303).
[^fn-p0317-2]: (†) *Immer leiser wird mein Schlummer.*
[^fn-p0317-3]: (‡) The words of the song *Wie Melodien zieht es* are by Klaus Groth. Brahms often succeeded in setting an abstract poem to a charming melody. Another example is Rückert’s *Mit vierzig Jahren*, Op. 94, No. 1.
[^fn-p0318-1]: (*) Amalie Joachim (*née* Schneeweiss, 1839-1899), whose professional name before she married was Amalie Weiss, was a contralto engaged at the Hanover *Hofoper* from 1862. She subsequently became famous as a *Liedersängerin*, and was unrivalled in her interpretation of Schumann’s songs. In 1863 she married Joachim. They went to live in Berlin in 1866, but separated in 1882.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0318-2]: (†) Brahms had forestalled this request, in a letter to Hermine Spies on November 4, by writing, half in jest, ‘ I actually dreamt that I heard you skip half a bar’s rest, and sing a crotchet instead of a quaver,’ to which the singer replied, ‘ It is very kind of you only to dream that I am unmusical. I have not only dreamt it, but known it for ages.’
[^fn-p0319-1]: (*) The person responsible for the breach in their correspondence.
[^fn-p0319-2]: (†) It is doubtful whether Brahms agreed with her objections to the chords of the six-four in *Immer leiser*. He evidently wrote them deliberately, because they seemed to him a fitting expression for the feverish exaltation of the song. Hanslick had suggested the poem (by Hermann Lingg), but at first neither contents nor form appealed to Brahms. The breaks in the song after ‘*singt im Wald*’ and ‘*Willst du mich*,’ which are really inadmissible, may be explained as expressing the failing of the invalid’s voice, which is making its last desperate efforts to be heard before sinking into a last sleep. Sung by an ideal interpreter, the song should produce the impression that it is costing the singer her life: for in response to the dying girl’s call comes, not her lover, but Death. Billroth, to whom Brahms sent the song on August 18 from Thun as ‘the work of one of your old colleagues’ (Hermann Lingg being a retired Bavarian army doctor), replied: ‘H. Lingg’s poem about the dying girl in your illuminating setting affected me most of all. I imagined it sung quite simply in a touching girlish voice, and I am not ashamed to say that I could not finish playing it for weeping.’ The ultimate success of this particular song justified Billroth’s choice, and the chain of chords of the six-four will go down to posterity unchallenged.
[^fn-p0320-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s Op. 27 and 42.
[^fn-p0320-2]: (†) The A major violin sonata.
[^fn-p0320-3]: (‡) Of his fourth symphony, which he was conducting at Buda-Pesth on the 22nd.
[^fn-p0321-1]: (*) Op. 18.
[^fn-p0322-1]: (*) Op. 52.
[^fn-p0322-2]: (†) Frau Bertha Hartmann, widow of the poet Moriz Hartmann, of Vienna, and a friend of Billroth’s.
[^fn-p0322-3]: (‡) From Goethe’s *Seasons* (‘Autumn,’ No. 69).
[^fn-p0322-4]: (§) See Letter 149, note.
[^fn-p0323-1]: (*) Op. 100 and Op. 101.
[^fn-p0323-2]: (†) Brahms always sent his manuscripts in an ordinary wrapper by book-post, if possible. Once, in Vienna, a friend brought him back the score of the E minor symphony which he had had to look at, and was horrified to see Brahms hurriedly tie it round with a piece of tape, and address it to Joachim just as it was. On his friend’s entreaty that he would register it, Brahms replied: ‘Nonsense! Stuff like this doesn’t get lost. If by chance it should, why, I should write out the score again, that’s all. All the same, I will be good, and register things in future.’ To send off a parcel with all the attendant formalities of sealing, filling in declaration forms, etc., was really a nightmare to the impatient composer. Pohl, and later Mandyczewski, were always willing to take it off his hands; but he did not care to give them the trouble, and always tried to despatch his things in the quickest and easiest way.
[^fn-p0323-3]: (‡) *Cf.* Letters 189-191.
[^fn-p0323-4]: (§) *Cf.* Letter 193.
[^fn-p0324-1]: (*) The second subject of the sonata in A (Op. 100) is a variant on the theme of *Wie Melodien zieht es*, and at the same time an affectionate remembrance of Klaus Groth. The song was composed before the sonata, and stands in the same relation to it as *Regenlied* (another Groth song) to the violin sonata in G. There is another reminiscence in the A major sonata—the touch of *Auf dem Kirchhofe* (Op. 105, No. 4) in the last movement.
[^fn-p0324-2]: (†) Schumann’s D minor symphony in the original version.
[^fn-p0327-1]: (*) *Legationsrat* Ernst von Wildenbruch (b. 1845), poet and novelist.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0327-2]: (†) To Frau Schumann.
[^fn-p0327-3]: (‡) *Im Herbst*, Op. 104, No. 5.
[^fn-p0328-1]: (*) Published later among the *Sechs Gesänge für gemischten Chor a capella*, Op. 57.
[^fn-p0329-1]: (*) Op. 97.
[^fn-p0329-2]: (†) Op. 100 and Op. 101.
[^fn-p0329-3]: (‡) The adventures of Diandar, the fisherman.
[^fn-p0329-4]: (§) Herzogenberg means the D minor *vivace*, which alternates three times with the *Andante tranquillo* in F major, and eventually closes the movement. It is slightly reminiscent in general colouring to Grieg’s violin sonata, Op. 8.
[^fn-p0330-1]: (*) P. 48, bar 18.
[^fn-p0330-2]: (†) P. 8, bar 1.
[^fn-p0330-3]: (‡) P. 8, last bar.
[^fn-p0330-4]: (§) Brahms divides it as follows: 3/4+2/4+2/4 (*Andante grazioso*).

[^fn-p0331-7]: (**) Herzogenberg’s chorus *In der Nacht*, from Op. 57.
[^fn-p0333-1]: (*) Frau Engelmann.
[^fn-p0333-2]: (†) The passage omitted consists of a violent tirade on the indecisive, opportunistic attitude of the *Musikalisches Wochenblatt*, and an urgent request that Brahms would interfere and set everyone to rights.
[^fn-p0333-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg, Op. 52.
[^fn-p0334-1]: (*) Brahms had just received this high distinction. The Order was founded by Frederick the Great, and has rarely found its way to the ‘wrong’ person.
[^fn-p0334-2]: (†) *Fünf Gesänge für gemischten Chor a capella*, published 1889.
[^fn-p0335-1]: (*) Paul Heyse and Gottfried Keller were among Brahms’s favourite poets.
[^fn-p0335-2]: (†) A portrait of Hans von Bülow adorning the note-paper.
[^fn-p0336-1]: (*) Brahms’s companions on his fifth Italian tour were Fritz Simrock, his publisher, and Theodor Kirchner, who came as Simrock’s guest. Writing to Bülow, Brahms says : ‘Simrock’s happy thought of giving Kirchner a glimpse of the promised land delighted me, but I now fear it is at least twenty years too late. At least, I suspect he only feels really at home when he sits down to dinner or supper, and can chat about the Gewandhaus and other splendours.’ The outward tour included Verona, Vicenza, Venice, Bologna, and Florence, the return being made by way of Pisa, Milan, and the St. Gothard to Thun, where Brahms arrived on May 15 for the summer.
[^fn-p0336-2]: (†) Brahms’s summer house at Hofstetten, near Thun, was beside the River Aar, at the point where it bends to flow into the town. There is a picture of the house, with its commemorative tablet, on plate xxv. of the *Brahms-Bilderbuch*, edited by Viktor von Miller.
[^fn-p0336-3]: (‡) Hanslick had raised a warning voice in the *Neue Freie Presse*, drawing the singer’s attention to various defects of style. It is true she had fallen a victim to an ‘East Prussian catarrh’ in between, and her voice had suffered considerable injury.
[^fn-p0338-1]: (*) Brahms had sent them a printed copy of the trio.
[^fn-p0338-2]: (†) *Im Herbst*, Op. 104, No. 5.
[^fn-p0339-1]: (*) The A major sonata.
[^fn-p0339-2]: (†) *Der Stern des Lieds*, ode for chorus and orchestra, Op. 55, and *Die Weihe der Nacht*, for contralto, chorus, and orchestra, Op. 56.
[^fn-p0339-3]: (‡) Of the publishing firm Rieter-Biedermann.
[^fn-p0340-1]: (*) He was probably not satisfied with his new compositions, and wanted to improve them.
[^fn-p0340-2]: (†) Brahms had promised his friend Wüllner, much against his will, as he insisted in a letter of many pages, to go to Cologne for the twenty-fourth *Tonkünstler-Versammlung*, where the following of his works were performed : *Darthulas Grabgesang* (from Op. 42), the *Triumphlied*, the violin concerto (Brodsky), and the new C minor trio, in which Brahms was associated with Holländer and Hegyesi. Richard Pohl, who plumed himself on ‘discovering’ Brahms on this occasion (*cf.* Kalbeck, *Brahms*, i. 216), described the trio in the *Wochenblatt* as ‘hardly among the most striking of his chamber-music works. On the way Brahms called on Frau Schumann, and removed a slight misunderstanding which had clouded their friendship temporarily.
[^fn-p0341-1]: (*) The double concerto for violin and ’cello.
[^fn-p0341-2]: (†) The words to *Stern des Lieds* and *Weihe der Nacht* are by Robert Hamerling and Friedrich Hebbel respectively.
[^fn-p0341-3]: (‡) Julius Spengel (b. 1853), composer and conductor of the Hamburg *Cäcilienverein*. In 1898 he published an interesting character study of Brahms, and in 1897 an essay, *Heinrich von Herzogenberg in seinen Vokalwerken*, published later by Rieter-Biedermann.
[^fn-p0342-1]: (*) Bach cantata.
[^fn-p0342-2]: (†) The whole sentence reads : ‘Was da lebte, was aus engem Kreise auf ins Weite strebte, sanft und leise sank es in sich selbst zurück’ (Hebbel).
[^fn-p0342-3]: (‡) ‘*Seele, du wachst noch*’ (Hebbel).
[^fn-p0342-4]: (§) Beethoven. Brahms places Hamerling’s ode on a level with Kotzebue’s poem *d’occasion*, which Beethoven set to music.
[^fn-p0342-5]: (‖) ‘Mag freudenleer hinziehn ein *Erkorener*.’
[^fn-p0343-1]: (*) The letter bore the postmark Thun ; but Frau von Herzogenberg was at Munich when it came, and therefore calls it a Munich letter.
[^fn-p0344-1]: (*) H. W. von Ziemssen, a Munich specialist.
[^fn-p0345-1]: (*) Op. 86, No. 3. Text by Max Kalbeck.
[^fn-p0345-2]: (†) Ps. xciv., for four soloists, double chorus, orchestra, and organ (Op. 60).
[^fn-p0345-3]: (‡) As a matter of fact, Brahms had sent the much-discussed chorus, together with other new compositions, to Billroth from Thun on August 18, 1886, Billroth being one of the few privileged to see his works before they were printed. Frau von Herzogenberg may have had her information from Frau Hartmann.
[^fn-p0346-1]: (*) Brahms had gone to Baden-Baden to meet Joachim and Hausmann on September 19, and rehearse the double concerto for violin and ’cello with the municipal orchestra at the *Kurhaus*, and also, as he expressed it in a letter to Frau Henriette Fritsch at Marseilles, to ‘practise hard.’ The first performance was announced for October 18 at Cologne, the second for November 18 at Frankfurt, and the third at Basel.
[^fn-p0346-2]: (†) Op. 102.
[^fn-p0348-1]: (*) Wüllner performed Herzogenberg’s *Die Weihe der Nacht* at the third Gürzenich concert, Cologne.
[^fn-p0349-1]: (*) At Meiningen, on December 25, Brahms conducted his Haydn *Variations*, the third symphony, and the B flat major piano concerto (D’Albert). On January 3 the double concerto was played there for the first time.
[^fn-p0349-2]: (†) Joachim and Hausmann played the double concerto at the Gewandhaus on January 1, Brahms being the conductor.
[^fn-p0350-1]: (*) Fräulein Marie Fillunger.
[^fn-p0351-1]: (*) Brahms kept her photograph there until his death.
[^fn-p0352-1]: (*) ‘Liseley,’ the house standing empty at Berchtesgaden.
[^fn-p0352-2]: (†) Dr. Theodor Gomperz, Professor of classical philology in Vienna.

[^fn-p0353-1]: (*) Refers to Letter 215.

[^fn-p0353-3]: (‡) Refers to Letter 215.
[^fn-p0354-1]: (*) ‘He delighteth not in the strength of the horse: he taketh not pleasure in the legs of a man’ (Ps. cxlvii. 10).—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0355-1]: (*) The Leipzig *Bachgesellschaft’s* publication.
[^fn-p0355-2]: (†) From the cantata for contralto solo, *Vergnügte Ruh’*, in the seventeenth book of cantatas.
[^fn-p0355-3]: (‡) Vol. xvi., No. 159.
[^fn-p0356-1]: (*) The hero in *Fidelio*, who was left to languish in a dungeon.
[^fn-p0356-2]: (†) A literary man, living at Munich, whose wife afterwards married Levi.
[^fn-p0356-3]: (‡) Hermann Levi (1839-1900), *Generalmusikdirektor* at Munich, was intimate with Brahms in the ’sixties and ’seventies.
[^fn-p0357-1]: (*) The symphony composed by Wagner in 1832 (*cf.* Letter 45, postscript).
[^fn-p0357-2]: (†) Heinrich Zöllner (b. 1854) produced a musical drama, founded on Goethe’s *Faust*, in 1887.
[^fn-p0358-1]: (*) Karoline Bettelheim, operatic singer, married Julius von Gomperz, President of the Chamber of Commerce at Brünnaud, brother of Professor Gomperz.
[^fn-p0359-1]: (*) *Gräfin* Wickenburg (*cf.* Letter 185).
[^fn-p0359-2]: (†) The death of Kaiser Wilhelm I. (March 9, 1888) had affected Brahms deeply also.
[^fn-p0359-3]: (‡) The celebration of his birthday, March 22.
[^fn-p0360-1]: (*) Kaiser Friedrich.
[^fn-p0360-2]: (†) The *Zigeunerlieder*, Op. 103.
[^fn-p0360-3]: (‡) She refers to her childlessness.
[^fn-p0361-1]: (*) The manuscript of the *Zigeunerlieder*.
[^fn-p0361-2]: (†) Brahms had persuaded Julius Allgeyer to write an essay on their mutually esteemed friend, Anselm Feuerbach, the painter (1829-1880).

    The essay, which subsequently served as a foundation for Allgeyer’s biography of Feuerbach (1894), was printed in the *Austrian Weekly Journal of Science and Art*, edited by Bruno Bucher, a friend of Brahms’s.
[^fn-p0364-1]: (*) The *Zigeunerlieder*.
[^fn-p0364-2]: (†) The words were taken from Hungarian volkslieder, and transcribed into German rhyme by Hugo Conrat, whose house in Vienna Brahms frequented a good deal at the time. The original melodies, with Conrat’s words underneath, were published at Buda-Pesth under the title *Ungarische Liebeslieder : 25 ungarische Volkslieder für mittlere Stimme. Klavierbegleitung von Zoltán Nagy.*
[^fn-p0365-1]: (*) Meaning Herzogenberg’s pupils.
[^fn-p0366-1]: (*) Professor Adolf Wach had gone to live near Interlaken.
[^fn-p0367-1]: (*) Wilhelm Hertz (1835-1902), Professor of Literature at the Munich Polytechnic, was a member of the Munich ‘Crocodile,’ a group of poets headed by Geibel, Heyse, and Lingg. His *Spielmannsbuch*, *Marie de France*, and *Tristan und Isolde*, were free adaptations in verse of old romantic and German originals.
[^fn-p0367-2]: (†) Karl Stauffer-Bern (1857-1891), painter, poet, and etcher (see Otto Brahms’s *Karl Stauffer-Bern: Sein Leben, Seine Briefe, Seine Gedichte’*).
[^fn-p0367-3]: (‡) Novel by Gottfried Keller, which had been coming out in the *Deutsche Rundschau*.
[^fn-p0368-1]: (*) *Zigeunerlieder.*
[^fn-p0368-2]: (†) No. 8.
[^fn-p0369-1]: (*) The card is addressed to Munich, Hess Strasse 30.
[^fn-p0369-2]: (†) Brahms had spent the month of May in Italy. It was his sixth visit to that country. He met a friend, Widmann, at Verona, who was able to act as guide to many hitherto unexplored towns. Brahms then returned with him to Thun for the summer. Widmann’s *Brahms-Erinnerungen* (pp. 144-158) includes a charming description of some incidents of their travels through Bologna, Rimini, San Marino, Ancona, Loretto, Rome (Frascati, Tivoli, and d’Anzio), and Florence.
[^fn-p0369-3]: (‡) Stuttgart.
[^fn-p0369-4]: (§) The double concerto (Op. 102) was performed by Joachim and Hausmann on the second day of the festival (June, 1888), under Immanuel Faisst’s direction.
[^fn-p0370-1]: (*) *Im Herbst*, Op. 104, No. 5.
[^fn-p0373-1]: (*) Karl Spitteler (b. 1845), a Swiss poet, writing under the pseudonym ‘Felix Tandem,’ had published several poems, and (since 1866) edited the *Schweizerische Grenzpost* at Basel. He made the acquaintance of Brahms through their mutual friend Widmann.
[^fn-p0373-2]: (†) The essay appeared in the *Grenzpost*, and was included later in Spitteler’s book *Lachende Wahrheiten*.
[^fn-p0375-1]: (*) Wife of Julius Röntgen. Her illness proved incurable.
[^fn-p0375-2]: (†) *Hofrat* Dr. Adolf Schmid.

[^fn-p0379-3]: (‡) Rudolf Westphal (1826-1892), theorist and writer on music.

In the repetition (p. 20, bar 21) the violin chords are marked *pizzicato* ; on the other hand, the violinist finds special legato marks in the beginning to show that the chords are to be played as broadly as possible, and, of course, with the bow. It is perfectly in keeping with the *con sentimento* which modifies the *un poco presto* of the signature.
