[^fn-p0394-1]: (*) Op. 108, p. 27, bars 3 and 4.
[^fn-p0394-2]: (†) Goethe, *Das Göttliche.*
[^fn-p0394-3]: (‡) Quotation from the song ‘*Wie Melodien zieht es,*’ Op. 105, No. 1.
[^fn-p0394-4]: (§) The letter is incomplete.
[^fn-p0395-1]: (*) The date was noted by Frau von Herzogenberg, and marks the *arrival* of the letter.
[^fn-p0395-2]: (†) Friedrich Nietzsche (1844-1900), the philosopher, had spent the previous winter at Nice, but was then in Turin.
[^fn-p0396-1]: (*) *Cf.* Letter 234.
[^fn-p0397-1]: (*) Appeared in 1887.
[^fn-p0397-2]: (†) *Der Fall Wagner, Turiner Brief vom Mai,* 1888. In this letter Nietzsche turns and rends his former idol. He concludes the letter with an attack on Brahms, culminating in the sentence (often misquoted): ‘His is the melancholy of impotence.’ But Nietzsche’s judgment was biassed by personal considerations in each case. Neither Wagner nor Brahms approved of his compositions.
[^fn-p0397-3]: (‡) Brahms.
[^fn-p0397-4]: (§) Published in 1886.
[^fn-p0397-5]: (‖) Nietzsche alludes to his friend Peter Gast (Heinrich Köselitz, b. 1854), composer of various operas, orchestral and chamber-music works. On June 28, 1888, Nietzsche expresses himself in a letter to the effect that Gast is the only man left whose music finds grace in his eyes, and that his opera (*The Lion of Venice*) is the first of modern operas—gay, emotional, and masterly in style, not amateurish like Wagner’s.
[^fn-p0398-1]: (*) *Hymnus an das Leben*, for chorus and orchestra, which Nietzsche had sent to Brahms with a dedication. Brahms, however, declined the honour, sending a polite message on his visiting card.
[^fn-p0399-1]: (*) *Cf.* Letter 239.
[^fn-p0399-2]: (†) ‘Perhaps the reverse may be true’ is a remark of Beethoven’s at the age of twenty, recorded on a sketch page for the music to Hölty’s ‘*Klage*’ (in possession of the *Archiv der Gesellschaft der Musikfreunde*, Vienna), as the result of a long debate on the relation between the method of notation and tempo. Brahms gleaned the phrase from a supplementary volume to the complete Beethoven edition, published in 1888, and was fond of using it to dispose of any sophistries and equivocations attributed to philosophers of the day. It is characteristic of his general attitude that he did not think it worth while to mention the incident between Nietzsche and himself.
[^fn-p0399-3]: (‡) Josef Labor (b. 1842), Court organist, pianist, and composer, in Vienna.
[^fn-p0401-1]: (*) The sculptor, who had been living at Florence since 1874.
[^fn-p0401-2]: (†) The third violin sonata.
[^fn-p0401-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg, Op. 60.
[^fn-p0401-4]: (§) At the Stern *Gesangverein*, Berlin.
[^fn-p0401-5]: (‖) Brahms’s house at Thun had been spoilt for him by a newly laid-out promenade on the bank of the Aare, which ran immediately under his windows. Strangers, especially English tourists, insisted on stopping to listen when he was playing.
[^fn-p0402-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s compositions—in particular Ps. xciv.
[^fn-p0402-2]: (†) The psalm begins, ‘O Lord God, to whom vengeance belongeth,’ but closes with the words, ‘Yea, the Lord our God shall cut them off.’
[^fn-p0403-1]: (*) An inn at Ischl.
[^fn-p0403-2]: (†) The music sent was probably Herzogenberg’s *Legenden* for piano and viola, Op. 62, dedicated to ‘his Friend, Josef Joachim.’
[^fn-p0403-3]: (‡) Three motets for four-part and eight-part chorus *a capella*, Op. 110, which Brahms had shown them on their visit to Ischl.
[^fn-p0404-1]: (*) At Baden-Baden.
[^fn-p0404-2]: (†) The motets mentioned in Letter 244. Brahms had followed his usual method of wrapping the manuscript in old Viennese dance-music, probably the polonaises referred to.
[^fn-p0404-3]: (‡) It is evident from this that Brahms had again indulged his passion for using old waste-paper.
[^fn-p0405-1]: (*) Of the different voices in the choruses.
[^fn-p0405-2]: (†) Meaning another page of old dance music.
[^fn-p0405-3]: (‡) *Fest- und Gedenksprüche*, Op. 109, and *Drei Motetten*, Op. 110, published by Simrock early in 1890.
[^fn-p0406-1]: (*) ‘*Wenn wir in höchsten Nöten seien,*’ and Op. 110, No. 2, ‘*Ach arme Welt, du trügest mich,*’ which Frau von Herzogenberg had appropriated as a Christmas present to herself.
[^fn-p0406-2]: (†) The piano arrangement is duly explained in the score as a ‘possible help in rehearsing.’
[^fn-p0406-3]: (‡) Brahms could not be induced to give up the missing page. Frau von Herzogenberg’s manuscript was found incomplete after her death.
[^fn-p0407-1]: (*) Brahms had thoroughly overhauled an early work (Trio in B, Op. 8), and brought it out in its improved form as a ‘new edition’ (see Kalbeck, *Brahms*, i. 156-163). He played it on February 22, 1890, in Vienna with Rosé and Hummer, and before that in Buda-Pesth with Hubay and Popper.
[^fn-p0407-2]: (†) Probably an anonymous discussion which appeared in the *Deutsche Kunst- und Musik-Zeitung*, a very inferior paper. The article, which was remarkable for its intelligence, took Brahms by surprise, and he praised it to a few of his friends. Among these was Mandyczewski, the writer of it; he kept his secret, however, so as not to spoil Brahms’s pleasure.
[^fn-p0408-1]: (*) Several of Herzogenberg’s compositions were performed on May 23 at the Hamburg *Tonkünstlerverein*, among them a string quartet (Op. 42, No. 3), the waltzes for pianoforte duet (Op. 53), and choruses for female voices from Op. 26 (performed by the *Singakademie* choir under Schwencke). The Herzogenbergs had gone over specially for this concert.
[^fn-p0410-1]: (*) Bismarck, who was then living in retirement at Friedrichsruh.
[^fn-p0411-1]: (*) Robert Kahn (b. 1865), composer and conductor, pupil of Vincenz Lachner, Kiel, Rheinberger, and Herzogenberg, at present Professor of Theory of Music at the *Hochschule*, Berlin. In the ’eighties he had the benefit of some lessons from Brahms.
[^fn-p0412-1]: (*) Quintet in G, Op. 111.
[^fn-p0413-1]: (*) P. 5, bar 7.
[^fn-p0413-2]: (†) Letter H in the score.
[^fn-p0413-3]: (‡) The second movement (*Grave ed appassionato*), Op. 88.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0414-1]: (*) Op. 111, p. 48, bar 9.
[^fn-p0414-2]: (†) P. 40, bar 15.
[^fn-p0414-3]: (‡) Op. 111, p. 48, bar 9.
[^fn-p0415-1]: (*) Op. 8 (*cf.* Letter 247, note).
[^fn-p0415-2]: (†) Quotation from the song *Heimweh* (Brahms, Op. 63, No. 9).
[^fn-p0415-3]: (‡) P. 4, bar 8 of original edition. The principal alterations were made in the development section.
[^fn-p0417-2]: (*) The appended invoice was enclosed in a parcel of music containing the B major trio (*Triolettchen*) and the G major quintet (*Quinkelei*). The words crossed out in the left-hand column were scored through with blue pencil.
[^fn-p0417-3]: (†) ‘To H. [German name for B natural] and E. von H.’—<span class="small-caps">TR</span>.
[^fn-p0417-1]: (1) Parts not available.
[^fn-p0417-4]: (‡) Op. 111.
[^fn-p0418-1]: (*) The revised trio in B, Op. 8.
[^fn-p0418-2]: (†) Widow of Moritz Hauptmann.
[^fn-p0418-3]: (‡) It was a question of whether the ’cello, which has the principal subject in the first movement, would be heard clearly through the semiquaver accompaniment (*forte*) of the other instruments, especially as a counter-melody of some importance begins in the third bar. The first performance of the piece was on November 11 in Vienna, at one of Arnold Rosé’s chamber-music evenings. Hummer, the ’cellist, despaired of making himself heard, in spite of the broad, vigorous tone for which he was famous, and Sigmund Bachrich, the first viola player, had the courage to point out to Brahms the necessity for some modification in the tone of the others. After playing the quintet in Berlin on December 11, Joachim wrote to Brahms: ‘And now the desired report as to the opening passage. After trying it in various ways, we came back to your original version, except for a slight modification of the *forte* from the end of the second bar, increasing the tone again later.’
[^fn-p0419-1]: (*) New compositions of Herzogenberg’s.
[^fn-p0419-2]: (†) Professor Adolf Exner, successor to Jhering at Vienna University, had handed over to Brahms the delightful correspondence between Jhering, his sister Marie Frisch, and Gottfried Keller, to look through before it was incorporated in Jakob Baechthold’s *Life of Keller* (vol. iii.). Brahms was so delighted with Keller’s lively wit that he would spend whole afternoons reading out extracts to his friends in Vienna, and even copied out some for his own use.
[^fn-p0420-1]: (*) Op. 111, p. 5, bar 3.
[^fn-p0421-1]: (*) P. 14, bar 3.
[^fn-p0421-2]: (†) *Cf.* Letter 252, ‘invoice.’
[^fn-p0421-3]: (‡) ‘*Brahms on the Prater*’ would be an ideal inscription for this quintet, which smacks both of Vienna and the North. A friend suggested it to Brahms after a rehearsal, and Brahms promptly replied, ‘You’ve hit it!’ adding, with a sly smile, ‘And all the pretty girls there, eh?’
[^fn-p0421-4]: (§) Title of a play by Ferdinand Raimund.
[^fn-p0422-1]: (*) Op. 111, p. 12, bar 9 (?).
[^fn-p0422-2]: (†) A poem by ——, which had been placed between the leaves of the Adagio by the author, to whom Brahms had lent the score.  It went to Berlin, and was returned in due course, Brahms’s attention being at last drawn to it by Frau von Herzogenberg.
[^fn-p0422-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg’s Requiem for four-part chorus and orchestra, Op. 72.
[^fn-p0422-4]: (§) Op. 111, Adagio, p. 27, bar 8.
[^fn-p0423-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s Requiem was performed at the Thomaskirche, Leipzig, on February 22, with great success.
[^fn-p0423-2]: (†) Herzogenberg’s psalm, Op. 71, for chorus and orchestra, written in honour of the Kaiser’s birthday, was performed on the same evening as the Requiem.
[^fn-p0423-3]: (‡) Karl Riedel (1827-1888). *Cf.* Letter 121.
[^fn-p0423-4]: (§) In bringing out Herzogenberg’s compositions.
[^fn-p0424-1]: (*) The manuscript of *Thirteen Canons for Women’s Voices*, published by Peters in 1891.
[^fn-p0424-2]: (†) The trio (B major) and quintet (G major) had been published in between by Simrock.
[^fn-p0425-1]: (*) The old Thomaskirche had just been thoroughly renovated.
[^fn-p0425-2]: (†) An allusion to his election to membership of the Royal Academy of Arts.
[^fn-p0425-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg had published an essay on Bischoff’s *Harmonielehre* in the *Vierteljahrsschrift für Musikwissenschaft* (1891, p. 267).
[^fn-p0426-1]: (*) Omitted by Brahms in the new edition.
[^fn-p0426-2]: (†) Some busybody, who professed to know all about Brahms and his plans.
[^fn-p0426-3]: (‡) Spitta took Herzogenberg’s Requiem as a basis for a historical critical essay on *Musikalische Seelenmessen*, afterwards incorporated in his book *Zur Musik* (Paetel, 1892).
[^fn-p0426-4]: (§) Spitta, in his edition of the works of Heinrich Schütz (1585-1672), had retained the original clefs, thereby rendering the score more difficult to read. Brahms always advocated the use of the soprano, alto, and tenor clefs, and used them in his own vocal scores ; but the mezzo-soprano and baritone clefs (as above) he considered obsolete, detesting them accordingly. He drew a sharp distinction between what he called ‘ antiquarian fads ’ and ‘ musical necessity.’ A vocal score written in four clefs gave him a much clearer idea than the modern contraction of two (treble and bass), and he defended his preference even against his publishers, who would have preferred to meet the public convenience in the matter.
[^fn-p0427-1]: (*) *Il primo libro dei Madrigali* (1611), by Heinrich Schütz.
[^fn-p0427-2]: (†) Adolf Schulze (b. 1835), singer, professor of singing at the *Hochschule*, Berlin.
[^fn-p0428-1]: (*) *Cf.* Letter 150.
[^fn-p0429-1]: (*) Brahms had received the news of Frau von Herzogenberg’s death (on January 7, 1892) by telegram.  No letters are in existence between May 10 and the present one.
[^fn-p0430-1]: (*) Subject of the violin sonata, Op. 78, which Brahms brought with him in manuscript to Salzburg in August, 1878 (*cf.* Letters 62 and 63).
[^fn-p0431-1]: (*) *Thirteen Canons*, Op. 113.
[^fn-p0431-2]: (†) After her death Herzogenberg published *Acht Klavierstücke*, by Elisabet von Herzogenberg, dedicating each of the eight pieces to one or other of her friends, including Frau Emma Engelmann-Brandes, Frau Lili Wach (*née* Mendelssohn-Bartholdy), Frau Hedwig von Holstein, Fräulein Helene Hauptmann, Fräulein Johanna Röntgen, and Frau Clara Schumann. No. 6 was left without a dedication, while No. 7 was dedicated to Frau Luise von Bezold-Engelmann by the composer before she died.
[^fn-p0432-1]: (*) Six quartets for soprano, alto, tenor, and bass, with pianoforte accompaniment (Op. 112).
[^fn-p0432-2]: (†) It was at San Remo that his wife died.
[^fn-p0433-1]: (*) Frau Henry Bennet-Brewster.
[^fn-p0433-2]: (†) Fräulein Else Billroth, a talented amateur musician, pupil of Stockhausen, living in Vienna.
[^fn-p0433-3]: (‡) Otto von Fleischl, doctor in Rome.
[^fn-p0433-4]: (§) Clarinet trio, Op. 114, and clarinet quintet, Op. 115, composed at Ischl in the summer of 1891.
[^fn-p0433-5]: (‖) *Träumereien an französische Kaminen*, by Richard Leander (v. Volkman).
[^fn-p0434-1]: (*) Hildebrand’s fine monument is carried out in early Renaissance style, and represents St. Cecilia (with the features of Frau von Herzogenberg) seated at the organ.
[^fn-p0434-2]: (†) In the canton of Appenzell, on Lake Constance, where Herzogenberg had built a house, *Zum Abendrot*.
[^fn-p0434-3]: (‡) *Cf.* Letter 266, note.
[^fn-p0435-1]: (*) Probably a Roman quartet society.
[^fn-p0435-2]: (†) Beethoven’s quartet, Op. 59, No. 1.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0435-3]: (‡) This was Brahms’s eighth and last Italian tour. He started on April 13, meeting his travelling companions, Josef Victor Widmann, of Berne, and Dr. Friedrich Hegar and the pianist Robert Freund, both of Zürich, at Milan, from whence they went to Genoa. The journey to Sicily was originally to have been made by boat, but Brahms did not care for long sea-journeys, and finally decided to go by train. On the way they stopped at Naples, Sorrento, Palermo, Girgenti, Catania, Syracuse, Taormina, Messina, Naples, and Venice. Brahms was back in Vienna on the 10th (*cf.* Widmann, *Johannes Brahms*, p. 163).
[^fn-p0436-1]: (*) Theodor Billroth died February 6, 1894, at Abbazia.
[^fn-p0436-2]: (†) Title of one of Gottfried Keller’s *Züricher Novellen*. The seven alluded to are probably Herzogenberg, Joachim, Hausmann, Spitta, Barth, Rudorff, and Adolf Schulze.
[^fn-p0436-3]: (‡) Hans von Bülow died at Cairo on February 12, 1894.
[^fn-p0437-1]: (*) Op. 118 and 119.
[^fn-p0437-2]: (†) Finger exercises, published in 1893 without opus number (*cf.* Letters 21, 77-79).
[^fn-p0437-3]: (‡) Op. 118, No. 3.
[^fn-p0438-1]: (*) Brahms was chiefly attracted to Austria and Vienna by the scenery, the city itself, and the people. He was never able to feel so much at home anywhere else.
[^fn-p0439-1]: (*) Two sonatas for clarinet and piano, Op. 120.
[^fn-p0439-2]: (†) *King Lear*, Act III., Scene iv.
[^fn-p0439-3]: (‡) Herzogenberg’s *Elegische Gesänge* (words by Eichendorff) for soprano, Op. 91.
[^fn-p0440-1]: (*) Herzogenberg’s *Die Geburt Christi*, Church oratorio for solos, mixed chorus, and children’s voices, accompanied by harmonium, strings, oboe, congregational singing, and organ (Op. 90).
[^fn-p0441-1]: (*) The tone of Brahms’s letter (Letter 274)—in particular, perhaps the expression ‘leisurely appreciation’ (Brahms wrote ‘*behaglichsten Geniessen*’ so indistinctly that Herzogenberg read ‘*behaglichen Genüssen*’)—wounded Herzogenberg so deeply as to lead to a serious breach between the friends. It will be seen that there is an interval of ten months between this letter and the next.—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0441-2]: (†) *Vier Ernste Gesänge*, Op. 121. Brahms had some qualms about publishing these songs, on account of the not only undogmatic, but in part incredible, texts to which they were composed. The new paper referred to is probably the *Monatsschrift für Gottesdienst und Kirchliche Kunst*, edited by Dr. Friedrich Spitta and Dr. Julius Smend, assisted by Herzogenberg.
[^fn-p0441-3]: (‡) Probably the posthumous *Choralvorspiele* for organ.
[^fn-p0441-4]: (§) Helene Hauptmann, daughter of Moritz Hauptmann, and an old friend of the Herzogenbergs, had undertaken to look after Herzogenberg and his house after his wife’s death.
[^fn-p0442-1]: (*) Helene Hauptmann.
[^fn-p0443-1]: (*) ‘O Death, how bitter is the remembrance of thee to a man that liveth at rest in his possessions . . . yea, unto him that is yet able to receive meat’ (Ecclesiasticus xli. 1).—<span class="small-caps">Tr.</span>
[^fn-p0444-1]: (*) The South German expression *Schnaderhüpfeln* is used to describe a lively song to which the harvesters dance at their festival. Brahms was fond of using it when alluding, either in speech or in writing, to his *Vier ernste Gesänge*, usually prefixing the adjective ‘godless.’
[^fn-p0444-2]: (†) Brahms was taken ill at Ischl in June with jaundice, which proved to be a symptom of the more serious organic disease which eventually led to his death. The Karlsbad treatment did him more harm than good.
[^fn-p0445-1]: (*) Herzogenberg dedicated his second piano quartet, Op. 95, to Brahms.
[^fn-p0445-2]: (†) On the day when this letter was written, Brahms had gone to bed ‘to rest a little.’ He never got up again, but died on April 3. A letter to thank Herzogenberg for this dedication was dictated to Arthur Faber, but is, unfortunately, not now available.
